**African Americans** or **Black Americans**, also formerly called **Afro-Americans**, are an [American racial and ethnic group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_and_ethnicity_in_the_United_States). As defined by the [United States census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_census), they are [Americans](19792942.md) who have ancestry from "any of the [Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_people) racial groups of [Africa](5334607.md)".[^cite_note-4] African Americans constitute the third-largest racial and ethnic group in the U.S., following [White Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Americans) and [Hispanic and Latino Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans).[^cite_note-5] In the ethnic group sense, the term "African American" generally denotes descendants of [Africans enslaved in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-Locke-6][^cite_note-wilkersonnyt-7] According to annual estimates from the [U.S. Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau), as of July 1, 2024, the overall Black population was estimated at 42,951,595, representing approximately 12.63% of the total [U.S. population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_the_United_States).[^cite_note-8]&#x20;

[African-American history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_history) began in the 16th century, when mainly West African and Central African slave traders sold African artisans, farmers, and warriors to [European slave traders](49005.md), who [transported them across the Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Passage) to the Western Hemisphere. They were [sold as slaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_colonial_history_of_the_United_States) to European colonists and put to work on [plantations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plantation), particularly [in the southern colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plantation_complexes_in_the_Southern_United_States). A few were able to achieve freedom through [manumission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manumission) or escape, and founded independent communities before and during the [American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution). After the United States was founded in 1783, most American [Black people continued to be enslaved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States), primarily in the [American South](179553.md), with four million enslaved people only [liberated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteenth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution) after the Northern victory over the South in the [Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War) of 1861 to 1865.[^cite_note-9]&#x20;

During [Reconstruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstruction_Era), African Americans gained [citizenship](31666.md) and adult males the [right to vote](31667.md); however, due to widespread belief in [White supremacy](41154041.md), they were treated as [second-class citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second-class_citizen) and soon [effectively disenfranchised in the South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disfranchisement_after_the_Reconstruction_era). These circumstances changed due to participation in the [military conflicts of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_African_Americans), substantial [migration out of the South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Migration_\(African_American\)), the elimination of legal [racial segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation_in_the_United_States), and the [civil rights movement](49001.md), which sought political and social freedom. However, [racism against African Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racism_against_African_Americans) and [racial socioeconomic disparity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_inequality_in_the_United_States) remain as problems into the 21st century. In the 20th and 21st centuries, immigration has played an increasingly significant role in the African-American community. As of 2022, 10% of the U.S. Black population were immigrants, and 20% were either immigrants or the children of immigrants.[^cite_note-10] While some Black immigrants or their children may also come to identify as African American, the majority of first-generation immigrants do not, preferring to identify with their nation of origin.[^cite_note-11][^cite_note-12] Most African Americans are of [West African](67393.md) and coastal [Central African](539606.md) ancestry, with varying amounts of [Western European](33800.md) and [Native American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States) ancestry (see [§Genetics](#Genetics)).&#x20;

[African-American culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_culture) has had a significant influence on worldwide culture, making numerous contributions to the [English language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_Vernacular_English), [literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_literature), [politics](#Politics), [cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul_food), [sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Americans_in_sports), and [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_music). The African-American contribution to popular music is so profound that most [American popular music](171080.md), including [gospel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_music), [blues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blues), [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz), [rock and roll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_and_roll), [R\&B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhythm_and_blues), [soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul_music), [funk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funk), [disco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disco), [hip-hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip-hop), and [house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_music) has its origins either partially or entirely in the African-American community.[^cite_note-13][^cite_note-14]&#x20;

## History

Main article: [African American history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_American_history)

See also: [African immigration to the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_immigration_to_the_United_States)

### Colonial era

Main article: [Slavery in the colonial history of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_colonial_history_of_the_United_States)

See also: [Atlantic slave trade](49005.md)



Major slave trading regions of Africa, 15th–19th centuries

The vast majority of those who were enslaved and transported in the [transatlantic slave trade](49005.md) were people from several [Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Africa) and [West African](67393.md) ethnic groups. They had been captured directly by European slave traders in coastal raids,[^cite_note-15] or captured and sold by West African slave traders or by half-European "merchant princes"[^cite_note-16] to European slave traders, who brought them to the Americas.[^cite_note-17]&#x20;

The first African slaves in what is now the United States arrived in the early 16th century. Africans were among [Juan Ponce de León](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Ponce_de_Le%C3%B3n)'s 1513 voyage that landed in what would become [Spanish Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Florida), and enslaved Africans arrived around the same time to [Spanish Puerto Rico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_settlement_of_Puerto_Rico).[^cite_note-18][^cite_note-19]&#x20;

Africans also came via [Santo Domingo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captaincy_General_of_Santo_Domingo) in the [Caribbean](18956035.md) to the [San Miguel de Gualdape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Miguel_de_Gualdape) colony (most likely located in the [Winyah Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winyah_Bay) area of present-day [South Carolina](27956.md)), founded by Spanish explorer [Lucas Vázquez de Ayllón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucas_V%C3%A1zquez_de_Ayll%C3%B3n) in 1526.[^cite_note-wright-20] The ill-fated colony was almost immediately disrupted by a fight over leadership, during which the slaves revolted and fled the colony to seek refuge among local [Native Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas). De Ayllón and many of the colonists died shortly afterward, due to an epidemic and the colony was abandoned. The settlers and the slaves who had not escaped returned to the Island of [Hispaniola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispaniola), whence they had come.[^cite_note-wright-20]&#x20;

The enslaved explorer [Esteban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estevanico) arrived in Florida with the [Narváez expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narv%C3%A1ez_expedition) in 1528, a journey that first landed in Santo Domingo and later traveled into [Spanish Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Texas) and the [Southwest](179571.md) before ending in Mexico.[^cite_note-21]&#x20;

The marriage between Luisa de Abrego, a free Black domestic servant from [Seville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seville), and Miguel Rodríguez, a White [Segovian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Segovia) conquistador in 1565 in [St. Augustine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Augustine,_Florida) (Spanish Florida), is the first known and recorded Christian marriage anywhere in what is now the continental United States.[^cite_note-22]&#x20;



[1619 : first Africans in Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Africans_in_Virginia)

The first recorded Africans in [English America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_America) (including most of the future United States) were ["20 and odd negroes"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Africans_in_Virginia) who arrived in [Jamestown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamestown,_Virginia), [Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_Virginia) via [Cape Comfort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Point_Comfort) in August 1619 as [indentured servants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indentured_servant).[^cite_note-23] As many Virginian settlers began to die from harsh conditions, more and more Africans were brought to work as laborers.[^cite_note-24]&#x20;

An indentured servant (who could be White or Black) would work for several years (usually four to seven) without wages. The status of indentured servants in early Virginia and Maryland was similar to slavery. Servants could be bought, sold, or leased, and they could be physically beaten for disobedience or attempting to running away. Unlike slaves, they were freed after their term of service expired or if their freedom was purchased. Their children did not inherit their status, and on their release from contract they received "a year's provision of corn, double apparel, tools necessary", and a small cash payment called "freedom dues".[^cite_note-25] Africans could legally raise crops and cattle to purchase their freedom.[^cite_note-26] They raised families, married other Africans and sometimes [intermarried](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interracial_marriage) with Native Americans or European [settlers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Settler).[^cite_note-27]&#x20;



The first slave auction at [New Amsterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Amsterdam) in 1655; illustration from 1895 by [Howard Pyle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Pyle)

[^cite_note-28]

By the 1640s and 1650s, several African families owned farms around [Jamestown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamestown,_Virginia), and some became wealthy by colonial standards and purchased indentured servants of their own. In 1640, the Virginia General Court recorded the earliest documentation of lifetime slavery when they sentenced [John Punch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Punch_\(slave\)), a Negro, to lifetime servitude under his master [Hugh Gwyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugh_Gwyn), for running away.[^cite_note-29][^cite_note-30]&#x20;

In [Spanish Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Florida), some [Spanish](963152.md) married or had [unions with](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Placage) [Pensacola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pensacola_people), [Creek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muscogee) or [African](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ethnic_groups_of_Africa) women, both enslaved and free, and their descendants created a mixed-race population of [mestizos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mestizo) and [mulattos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mulatto). The Spanish encouraged slaves from the [colony of Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Georgia) to come to Florida as a refuge, promising freedom in exchange for conversion to [Catholicism](606848.md). [King Charles II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_II_of_Spain) issued a royal proclamation freeing all slaves who fled to Spanish Florida and accepted conversion and baptism. Most went to the area around [St. Augustine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Augustine,_Florida), but escaped slaves also reached Pensacola. St. Augustine had mustered an all-Black [militia](20622.md) unit defending Spanish Florida as early as 1683.[^cite_note-31]&#x20;



Black slaves working on a [tobacco plantation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plantation_complexes_in_the_Southern_United_States) in the [colony of Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_Virginia), 1759

One of the Dutch African arrivals, [Anthony Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Johnson_\(American_Colonial\)), would later own one of the first Black "slaves", [John Casor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Casor), resulting from the court ruling of a civil case.[^cite_note-russell-32][^cite_note-Sweet2005-33]&#x20;

The popular conception of a race-based slave system did not fully develop until the 18th century. The [Dutch West India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_West_India_Company) introduced slavery in 1625 with the importation of eleven Black slaves into [New Amsterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Amsterdam) (present-day New York City). All the colony's slaves, however, were freed upon its surrender to the English.[^cite_note-branchandroot-34]&#x20;

[Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massachusetts_Bay_Colony) was the first English colony to legally recognize slavery in 1641. In 1662, Virginia passed a law that children of enslaved women would take the status of the mother, rather than that of the father, as was the case under [common law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_law). This legal principle was called *[partus sequitur ventrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partus_sequitur_ventrum)*.[^cite_note-Banks-35][^cite_note-36]&#x20;

By an act of 1699, Virginia ordered the deportation of all free Blacks, effectively defining all people of African descent who remained in the colony as slaves.[^cite_note-Wood-37] In 1670, the colonial assembly passed a law prohibiting free and baptized Blacks (and Native Americans) from purchasing Christians (in this act meaning White Europeans) but allowing them to buy people "of their owne nation".[^cite_note-38]&#x20;



1774 image of a [fugitive slave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fugitive_slaves_in_the_United_States) in a New York newspaper, offering a \$10 reward (equivalent to \$295 in 2025). Slave owners, including [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington) and [Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson), placed around 200,000 runaway slave adverts in newspapers across the US before slavery ended in 1865.

[^cite_note-Runaway-39]

[^cite_note-Fugitives-40]

In [Spanish Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_\(New_Spain\)), although there was no movement toward abolition of the African slave trade, Spanish rule introduced a new law called [*coartación*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coartaci%C3%B3n_\(slavery\)), which allowed slaves to buy their freedom, and that of others.[^cite_note-41] Although some did not have the money to do so, government measures on slavery enabled the existence of many free Blacks. This caused problems to the Spaniards with the [French creoles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_creoles) (French who had settled in [New France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_France)) who had also populated Spanish Louisiana. The French creoles cited that measure as one of the system's worst elements.[^cite_note-louisiana-42]&#x20;

First established in South Carolina in 1704, groups of armed White men—[slave patrols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_patrol)—were formed to monitor enslaved Black people.[^cite_note-Patrols-43] Their function was to police slaves, especially fugitives. Slave owners feared that slaves might organize revolts or [slave rebellions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_rebellion), so state militias were formed to provide a military command structure and discipline within the slave patrols. These patrols were used to detect, encounter, and crush any organized slave meetings which might lead to revolts or [rebellions](513246.md).[^cite_note-Patrols-43]&#x20;

The earliest African American congregations and churches were organized before 1800 in both northern and southern cities following the [Great Awakening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Great_Awakening). By 1775, Africans made up 20% of the population in the [American colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteen_Colonies), which made them the second-largest ethnic group after [English Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Americans).[^cite_note-44]&#x20;

### From the American Revolution to the Civil War

Main article: [Slavery in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States)



[Crispus Attucks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crispus_Attucks), the first "[martyr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martyr_\(politics\))" of the [American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution). He was of [Native American and African American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Indians_in_the_United_States) descent.

During the 1770s, Africans, both enslaved and free, helped rebellious American colonists secure their independence by defeating the British in the [American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War).[^cite_note-45] Blacks played a role in both sides in the American Revolution. Activists in the Patriot cause included [James Armistead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Armistead), [Prince Whipple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_Whipple), and [Oliver Cromwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oliver_Cromwell_\(American_soldier\)).[^cite_note-46][^cite_note-47] Around 15,000 [Black Loyalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Loyalist) left with the British after the war, most of them ending up as free Black people in England[^cite_note-48] or its colonies, such as the [Black Nova Scotians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Nova_Scotians) and the [Sierra Leone Creole people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sierra_Leone_Creole_people).[^cite_note-Duke_Law-49][^cite_note-Walker-50]&#x20;

In the [Spanish Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Louisiana), Governor [Bernardo de Gálvez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernardo_de_G%C3%A1lvez) organized Spanish free Black men into two militia companies to defend [New Orleans](53842.md) during the American Revolution. They fought in the 1779 battle in which Spain captured [Baton Rouge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baton_Rouge) from the British. Gálvez also commanded them in campaigns against the British outposts in [Mobile, Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobile,_Alabama), and [Pensacola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pensacola), Florida. He recruited slaves for the militia by pledging to free anyone who was seriously wounded and promised to secure a low price for *coartación* (buy their freedom and that of others) for those who received lesser wounds. During the 1790s, Governor [Francisco Luis Héctor, baron of Carondelet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Luis_H%C3%A9ctor_de_Carondelet) reinforced local fortifications and recruit even more free Black men for the militia. Carondelet doubled the number of free Black men who served, creating two more militia companies—one made up of Black members and the other of [pardo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pardo) (mixed race). Serving in the militia brought free Black men one step closer to equality with Whites, allowing them, for example, the right to carry arms and boosting their earning power. However, actually these privileges distanced free Black men from enslaved Blacks and encouraged them to identify with Whites.[^cite_note-louisiana-42]&#x20;



*Nat Turner & His Confederates in Conference* during the [slave rebellion of 1831](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nat_Turner%27s_Rebellion)

Slavery had been tacitly enshrined in the [US Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Constitution) through provisions such as Article I, Section 2, Clause 3, commonly known as the [3/5 compromise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3/5_compromise). Due to the restrictions of [Section 9, Clause 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_One_of_the_United_States_Constitution#Clause_1:_Slave_trade), Congress was unable to pass an [Act Prohibiting Importation of Slaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Act_Prohibiting_Importation_of_Slaves) until 1807.[^cite_note-51] [Fugitive slave laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fugitive_slave_laws_in_the_United_States) (derived from the [Fugitive Slave Clause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fugitive_Slave_Clause) of the Constitution—[Article IV, Section 2, Clause 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_Four_of_the_United_States_Constitution#Clause_3:_Fugitive_Slave_Clause)) were passed by Congress in both [1793](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fugitive_Slave_Act_of_1793) and [1850](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fugitive_Slave_Act_of_1850), guaranteeing the right of a slaveholder to recover an escaped slave anywhere within the US.[^cite_note-Fugitives-40] Slave owners, who viewed slaves as property, ensured that it became a federal crime to aid or assist those who had fled "from the service of their masters" per the 1793 Act signed by Washington, or to interfere with their capture.[^cite_note-Runaway-39] By that time, slavery, which almost exclusively targeted Black people, had become the most critical and contentious political issue in the [Antebellum United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antebellum_United_States), repeatedly sparking crises and conflicts. Among these were the [Missouri Compromise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri_Compromise), the [Compromise of 1850](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compromise_of_1850), the infamous [Dred Scott decision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dred_Scott_decision), and [John Brown's raid on Harpers Ferry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Brown%27s_raid_on_Harpers_Ferry).&#x20;



[Frederick Douglass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_Douglass) (c. 1850) formerly enslaved, who became an [abolitionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_States) and statesman

Prior to the [Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War), eight serving presidents had owned slaves, a practice that was legally protected under the US Constitution.[^cite_note-52] By 1860, the number of enslaved Black people in the US had grown to between 3.5 and 4.4 million, largely as a result of the [Atlantic slave trade](49005.md). In addition, 488,000–500,000 Black people lived free around the country, with legislated limits[^cite_note-ACS-53][^cite_note-GomezPremdas-54] imposed upon them in addition to "unconquerable prejudice" from Whites according to [Henry Clay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Clay).[^cite_note-55] In response to these conditions, some free Black people chose to leave the US and emigrate to [Liberia](17791.md) in West Africa.[^cite_note-ACS-53] Liberia had been established in 1821 as a settlement by the [American Colonization Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Colonization_Society) (ACS), with many abolitionist members of the ACS believing Black Americans would have greater opportunities for freedom and equality in Africa than they would in the US.[^cite_note-ACS-53]&#x20;

Slaves not only represented a significant financial investment for their owners, but they also played a crucial role in producing the country's most valuable product and export: [cotton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Cotton). Enslaved people were instrumental in the construction of several prominent structures such as, the [United States Capitol](31979.md), the [White House](33057.md) and other [Washington, D.C.–based](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_District_of_Columbia) buildings.[^cite_note-56] Similar building projects existed in the [slave states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_states_and_free_states).&#x20;



*Slaves Waiting for Sale: Richmond, Virginia*, 1853. Note the new clothes. The [domestic slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_slave_trade) broke up many families, and individuals lost their connection to families and clans.

By 1815, the [domestic slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States#Domestic_slave_trade_and_forced_migration) had become a significant and major economic activity in the United States, continuing to flourish until the 1860s.[^cite_note-CUP-57] Historians estimate that nearly one million individuals were subjected to this forced migration, which was often referred to as a new "Middle Passage". The historian [Ira Berlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ira_Berlin) described this internal forced migration of enslaved people as the "central event" in the life of a slave during the period between the American Revolution and the Civil War. Berlin emphasized that whether enslaved individuals were directly uprooted or lived in constant fear that they or their families would be involuntarily relocated, "the massive deportation traumatized Black people" throughout the US.[^cite_note-58] As a result of this large-scale forced movement, countless individuals lost their connection to families and clans, and many ethnic Africans lost their knowledge of varying tribal origins in Africa.[^cite_note-CUP-57]&#x20;

> For me, my 'X' replaced the white [slavemaster name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_name) of 'Little' which some blue-eyed devil named Little had imposed upon my paternal forebears.

— [Malcolm X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malcolm_X) explaining in his 1965 autobiography that the "X" symbolized the true African family name that he could never know.[^cite_note-59]



[Harriet Tubman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harriet_Tubman) (c. 1869) : after escaping [slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States), she made some 13 missions to rescue approximately 70 enslaved people

The 1863 photograph of [Wilson Chinn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilson_Chinn), a branded slave from Louisiana, along with the famous image of [Gordon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gordon_\(slave\)) and his scarred back, served as two of the earliest and most powerful examples of how the newborn medium of photography could be used to visually document and encapsulate the brutality and cruelty of slavery.[^cite_note-60]&#x20;

Emigration of free Blacks to their continent of origin had been proposed since the Revolutionary war. After [Haiti became independent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Revolution), it tried to recruit African Americans to migrate there after it re-established trade relations with the United States. The Haitian Union was a group formed to promote relations between the countries.[^cite_note-Nikki-61] After riots against Blacks in [Cincinnati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cincinnati), its Black community sponsored founding of the [Wilberforce Colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilberforce_Colony), an initially successful settlement of African American immigrants to Canada. The colony was one of the first such independent political entities. It lasted for a number of decades and provided a destination for about 200 Black families emigrating from a number of locations in the United States.[^cite_note-Nikki-61]&#x20;



[Colored Troops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Colored_Troops) of the [Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Army) during the [Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War), 1865

In 1863, during the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War), President [Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln) signed the [Emancipation Proclamation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emancipation_Proclamation). The proclamation declared that all slaves in Confederate-held territory were free.[^cite_note-62] Advancing Union troops enforced the proclamation, with Texas being the last state to be emancipated, in 1865.[^cite_note-63]&#x20;

Slavery in a few border states continued until the ratification of the [Thirteenth Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteenth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution) in December 1865.[^cite_note-64] While the [Naturalization Act of 1790](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naturalization_Act_of_1790) limited US citizenship to Whites only,[^cite_note-Schultz-65][^cite_note-Sato-66] the [14th Amendment](31666.md) (1868) gave Black people citizenship, and the [15th Amendment](31667.md) (1870) gave Black men the right to vote.[^cite_note-67]&#x20;

### Reconstruction era and Jim Crow

Main articles: [Reconstruction era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstruction_era) and [Jim Crow laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Crow_laws)



*Education at Last*. From 1865 to 1872, thousands of newly freed slaves, both young and old, received food, clothing, medical care, and previously denied education, via the War Department's Freedmen's Bureau.

African Americans quickly set up congregations for themselves, as well as schools and community/civic associations, to have space away from White control or oversight. While the post-war Reconstruction era was initially a time of progress for African Americans, that period ended in 1876. By the late 1890s, Southern states enacted Jim Crow laws to enforce [racial segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation) and [disenfranchisement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disfranchisement_after_the_American_Civil_War).[^cite_note-68]&#x20;



African American man drinking from a [segregated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation_in_the_United_States) water fountain reserved for "[Colored](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colored)", July 1939.

Segregation was imposed with signs showing Blacks where they could legally walk, talk, drink, rest, or eat.[^cite_note-Leon_Litwack_2004-69] For those places that were racially mixed, non-Whites had to wait until all White customers were dealt with.[^cite_note-Leon_Litwack_2004-69] Most African Americans obeyed the segregation laws, to avoid [racially motivated violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_violence). To maintain self-esteem and dignity, African Americans such as [Anthony Overton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Overton) and [Mary McLeod Bethune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_McLeod_Bethune) continued to build their own [schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historically_Black_colleges_and_universities), [churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_church), banks, social clubs, and other businesses.[^cite_note-70]&#x20;

In the last decade of the 19th century, racially discriminatory laws and racial violence aimed at African Americans began to mushroom in the United States, a period often referred to as the "[nadir of American race relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nadir_of_American_race_relations)". These discriminatory acts included racial segregation—upheld by the United States Supreme Court decision in *[Plessy v. Ferguson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plessy_v._Ferguson)* in 1896—which was legally mandated by southern states and nationwide at the local level of government, [voter suppression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_suppression_in_the_United_States) or disenfranchisement in the southern states, denial of economic opportunity or resources nationwide, and private acts of violence and mass racial violence aimed at African Americans unhindered or encouraged by government authorities.[^cite_note-71]&#x20;

### Great migration and civil rights movement

Main articles: [Great Migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Migration_\(African_American\)) and [civil rights movement](49001.md)



A group of White men pose for a photograph as they stand over the Black victim, Will Brown, who had been [lynched](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynched) and had his body mutilated and burned during the [Omaha race riot of 1919](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omaha_race_riot_of_1919) in [Omaha, Nebraska](46159.md). Postcards and photographs of lynchings were popular souvenirs in the US.

[^cite_note-72]

The desperate conditions of African Americans in the South sparked the [Great Migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Migration_\(African_American\)) during the first half of the 20th century which led to a growing African American community in [Northern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_United_States) and Western United States.[^cite_note-73] The rapid influx of Blacks disturbed the racial balance within Northern and Western cities, exacerbating hostility between both Blacks and Whites in the two regions.[^cite_note-74] The [Red Summer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Summer) of 1919 was marked by hundreds of deaths and higher casualties across the US as a result of race riots that occurred in more than three dozen cities, such as the [Chicago race riot of 1919](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_race_riot_of_1919) and the [Omaha race riot of 1919](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omaha_race_riot_of_1919). Overall, Blacks in Northern and Western cities experienced [systemic discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racism_against_Black_Americans) in a plethora of aspects of life. Within employment, economic opportunities for Blacks were routed to the lowest-status and restrictive in potential mobility. At the 1900 [Hampton Negro Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hampton_Negro_Conference), Reverend Matthew Anderson said: "...the lines along most of the avenues of wage earning are more rigidly drawn in the North than in the South."[^cite_note-75] Within the housing market, stronger discriminatory measures were used in correlation to the influx, resulting in a mix of "targeted violence, [restrictive covenants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusionary_covenants), [redlining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redlining) and [racial steering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_steering)".[^cite_note-76] While many Whites defended their space with violence, intimidation, or legal tactics toward African Americans, many other Whites migrated to more racially homogeneous suburban or exurban regions, a process known as [White flight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_flight).[^cite_note-77]&#x20;

Despite discrimination, drawing cards for leaving the hopelessness in the South were the growth of African American institutions and communities in Northern cities. Institutions included Black oriented organizations (e.g., [Urban League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_League), [NAACP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAACP)), churches, businesses, and newspapers, as well as successes in the development in African American intellectual culture, music, and popular culture (e.g., [Harlem Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlem_Renaissance), [Chicago Black Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Black_Renaissance)). The [Cotton Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotton_Club) in Harlem was a Whites-only establishment, with Blacks (such as [Duke Ellington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_Ellington)) allowed to perform, but to a White audience.[^cite_note-78] Black Americans also found a new ground for political power in Northern cities, without the enforced disabilities of [Jim Crow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Crow).[^cite_note-79][^cite_note-80]&#x20;



[Rosa Parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosa_Parks) being fingerprinted after being arrested for not giving up her seat on a bus for a white passenger to sit in the otherwise-empty row

By the 1950s, the [civil rights movement](49001.md) was gaining momentum. A 1955 lynching that sparked public outrage about injustice was that of [Emmett Till](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmett_Till), a 14-year-old boy from Chicago. Spending the summer with relatives in [Money, Mississippi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Money,_Mississippi), Till was killed for allegedly having [wolf-whistled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf-whistle) at a White woman. Till had been badly beaten, one of his eyes was gouged out, and he was shot in the head. The visceral response to his mother's decision to have an open-casket funeral mobilized the Black community throughout the US.[^cite_note-Atlantic-81] [Vann Newkirk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vann_Newkirk) wrote "the trial of his killers became a pageant illuminating the tyranny of [White supremacy](33178.md)".[^cite_note-Atlantic-81] The state of Mississippi tried two defendants, but they were speedily acquitted by an [all-White jury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-White_jury).[^cite_note-82] One hundred days after Emmett Till's murder, [Rosa Parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosa_Parks) refused to give up her seat on the bus in Alabama—indeed, Parks told Emmett's mother [Mamie Till](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamie_Till) that "the photograph of Emmett's disfigured face in the casket was set in her mind when she refused to give up her seat on the Montgomery bus."[^cite_note-83]&#x20;



[March on Washington for Jobs and Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_on_Washington_for_Jobs_and_Freedom), August 28, 1963, shows civil rights leaders and union leaders.

The [March on Washington for Jobs and Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_on_Washington_for_Jobs_and_Freedom) and the conditions which brought it into being are credited with putting pressure on presidents [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_John_F._Kennedy) and [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Lyndon_B._Johnson). Johnson put his support behind passage of the [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964) that banned discrimination in public accommodations, employment, and [labor unions](17626.md), and the [Voting Rights Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_Rights_Act) of 1965, which expanded federal authority over states to ensure Black political participation through protection of voter registration and elections.[^cite_note-84] By 1966, the emergence of the [Black Power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Power) movement, which lasted from 1966 to 1975, expanded upon the aims of the civil rights movement to include economic and political self-sufficiency, and freedom from White authority.[^cite_note-abbeville-85]&#x20;

During the post-war period, many African Americans continued to be economically disadvantaged relative to other Americans. Average Black income stood at 54 percent of that of White workers in 1947, and 55 percent in 1962. In 1959, median family income for Whites was \$5,600 (equivalent to \$61,849 in 2025), compared with \$2,900 (equivalent to \$32,029 in 2025) for non-White families. In 1965, 43 percent of all Black families fell into the poverty bracket, earning under \$3,000 (equivalent to \$30,649 in 2025) a year. The 1960s saw improvements in the social and economic conditions of many Black Americans.[^cite_note-ReferenceA-86]&#x20;

From 1965 to 1969, Black family income rose from 54 to 60 percent of White family income. In 1968, 23 percent of Black families earned under \$3,000 (equivalent to \$27,775 in 2025) a year, compared with 41 percent in 1960. In 1965, 19 percent of Black Americans had incomes equal to the national median, a proportion that rose to 27 percent by 1967. In 1960, the median level of education for Blacks had been 10.8 years, and by the late 1960s, the figure rose to 12.2 years, half a year behind the median for Whites.[^cite_note-ReferenceA-86]&#x20;

### Post–civil rights era

Main article: [Post–civil rights era in African-American history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post%E2%80%93civil_rights_era_in_African-American_history)

Politically and economically, African Americans have made substantial strides during the post–civil rights era. In 1967, [Thurgood Marshall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thurgood_Marshall) became the [first African American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_African-American_federal_judges) Supreme Court Justice. In 1968, [Shirley Chisholm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shirley_Chisholm) became the first Black woman elected to the [US Congress](31756.md). In 1989, [Douglas Wilder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Wilder) became the first African American elected governor in US history. [Clarence Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarence_Thomas) succeeded Marshall to become the second African American Supreme Court Justice in 1991. In 1992, [Carol Moseley-Braun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carol_Moseley-Braun) of [Illinois](14849.md) became the first African American woman elected to the [US Senate](24909346.md). There were 8,936 Black officeholders in the United States in 2000, showing a net increase of 7,467 since 1970. In 2001, there were 484 Black mayors.[^cite_note-87]&#x20;



[U.S. President](24113.md) [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama)'s official photograph in the [Oval Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oval_Office) in 2012

In 2005, the number of Africans immigrating to the United States, in a single year, surpassed the peak number who were involuntarily brought to the United States during the [Atlantic slave trade](49005.md).[^cite_note-88] On November 4, 2008, [Democratic](5043544.md) [Senator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senator) [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama)—the son of a White American mother and a Kenyan father—[defeated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_United_States_presidential_election) [Republican](32070.md) Senator [John McCain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_McCain) to become the first African American to be elected president. At least 95 percent of African American voters voted for Obama.[^cite_note-CNN-Obama-89][^cite_note-Politico-Obama-90] He also received overwhelming support from young and educated Whites, a majority of [Asians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Americans),[^cite_note-exitpoll-91] and [Hispanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans),[^cite_note-exitpoll-91] picking up a number of new states in the Democratic electoral column.[^cite_note-CNN-Obama-89][^cite_note-Politico-Obama-90] Obama lost the overall White vote, although he won a larger proportion of White votes than any previous non-incumbent Democratic presidential candidate since [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter).[^cite_note-92] Obama was [reelected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_United_States_presidential_election) for a second and [final term](505825.md), by a similar margin on November 6, 2012.[^cite_note-93] In 2021, [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris), the daughter of a Jamaican father and Indian mother, became the first woman, the first African American, and the first [Asian American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_American) to serve as [Vice President of the United States](32759.md).[^cite_note-94] In June 2021, [Juneteenth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juneteenth), a day which commemorates the end of slavery in the US, became a federal holiday.[^cite_note-Juneteenth-95]&#x20;

## Demographics

Further information: [Historical racial and ethnic demographics of the United States § Black population as a percentage of the total population by U.S. region and state (1790–2010)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_racial_and_ethnic_demographics_of_the_United_States#Black_population_as_a_percentage_of_the_total_population_by_U.S._region_and_state_\(1790%E2%80%932010\)), [List of U.S. communities with African-American majority populations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._communities_with_African-American_majority_populations), [List of U.S. counties with African-American majority populations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._counties_with_African-American_majority_populations), and [List of U.S. states by African-American population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_by_African-American_population)



Black Americans (alone) population pyramid in 2020



Proportion of African Americans in each US state, Washington, D.C., and Puerto Rico as of the 2020 United States Census



Proportion of [Black Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Americans) (alone or in combination) in each county of the [fifty states](12610470.md), [Washington, D.C.](108956.md), and [Puerto Rico](23041.md) as of the [2020 United States census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_census)



[Majority Black American counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_majority-Black_counties_in_the_United_States) in the United States according to the [2020 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_census)



US census map indicating US counties with fewer than 25 Black or African American inhabitants



Graph showing the percentage of the African American population living in the American South, 1790–2010. Note [the major declines between 1910 and 1940](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Migration_\(African_American\)) and [1940–1970](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Great_Migration_\(African_American\)), and [the reverse trend post-1970](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Great_Migration). Nonetheless, the absolute majority of the African American population has always lived in the American South.

In 1790, when the [first US census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1790_United_States_census) was taken, Africans (including slaves and free people) numbered about 760,000—about 19.3% of the population. In 1860, at the start of the [Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War), the African American population had increased to 4.4 million, but the percentage rate dropped to 14% of the overall population of the country. The vast majority were slaves, with only 488,000 counted as "[freemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedman)". By 1900, the Black population had doubled and reached 8.8 million.[^cite_note-96]&#x20;

In 1910, about 90% of African Americans lived in the South. Large numbers began migrating north looking for better job opportunities and living conditions, and to escape [Jim Crow laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Crow_laws) and racial violence. The [Great Migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Migration_\(African_American\)), as it was called, spanned the 1890s to the 1970s. From 1916 through the 1960s, more than six million [Black people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_people) moved north. But in the 1970s and 1980s, [that trend reversed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Great_Migration), with more African Americans moving south to the [Sun Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sun_Belt) than leaving it.[^cite_note-97]&#x20;

The African American population in the United States declined over time as a percentage of the total population until 1930, and has been rising since then:&#x20;

By 1990, the African American population reached about 30 million and represented 12% of the US population, roughly the same proportion as in 1900.[^cite_note-99]&#x20;

At the time of the [2000 US census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_United_States_census), 54.8% of African Americans lived in the [South](179553.md). In that year, 17.6% of African Americans lived in the [Northeast](431669.md) and 18.7% in the [Midwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwestern_United_States), while only 8.9% lived in the [Western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_United_States) states. The west does have a sizable Black population in certain areas, however. California, the nation's most populous state, has the fifth-largest African American population, only behind New York, Texas, Georgia, and Florida. According to the 2000 census, approximately 2.05% of [African Americans identified as Hispanic or Latino in origin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans),[^cite_note-tthqvu-101] many of whom may be of [Brazilian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Brazilians), [Puerto Rican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro%E2%80%93Puerto_Ricans), [Dominican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominican_Americans), [Cuban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Cubans), [Haitian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Americans), or other [Latin American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro%E2%80%93Latin_Americans) descent. The only self-reported *ancestral* groups larger than African Americans are the [Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Americans) and [Germans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Americans).[^cite_note-102]&#x20;



[Band rehearsal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marching_band) on [125th Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/125th_Street_\(Manhattan\)) in [Harlem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlem), the historic epicenter of African American culture. New York City is home by a significant margin to the world's largest [Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_people) population of any city outside [Africa](5334607.md), at over 2.2 million. [African immigration to New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_immigration_to_the_United_States) is now driving the growth of the city's Black population.

[^cite_note-AfricanMigrationNYC-103]

According to the [2010 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_United_States_census), nearly 3% of people who self-identified as Black had recent ancestors who immigrated from another country. Self-reported [non-Hispanic Black immigrants from the Caribbean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Indian_Americans), mostly from Jamaica and Haiti, represented 0.9% of the US population, at 2.6 million.[^cite_note-factfinder2.census.gov-104] Self-reported Black immigrants from sub-Saharan Africa also represented 0.9%, at about 2.8 million.[^cite_note-factfinder2.census.gov-104] Additionally, self-identified [Black Hispanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans) represented 0.4% of the United States population, at about 1.2 million people, largely found within the Puerto Rican and Dominican communities.[^cite_note-105] Self-reported Black immigrants hailing from other countries in the Americas, such as Brazil and Canada, as well as several European countries, represented less than 0.1% of the population. Mixed-race Hispanic and non-Hispanic Americans who identified as being part Black, represented 0.9% of the population. Of the 12.6% of United States residents who identified as Black, around 10.3% were "native Black American" or ethnic African Americans, who are direct descendants of West/Central Africans brought to the US as slaves. These individuals make up well over 80% of all Blacks in the country. When including [people of mixed-race origin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiracial_Americans), about 13.5% of the US population self-identified as Black or "mixed with Black".[^cite_note-106] However, according to the US Census Bureau, evidence from the 2000 census indicates that many African and Caribbean immigrant ethnic groups do not identify as "Black, African Am., or Negro". Instead, they wrote in their own respective ethnic groups in the "Some Other Race" write-in entry. As a result, the census bureau devised a new, separate "African American" ethnic group category in 2010 for ethnic African Americans.[^cite_note-Tcpms-107] [Nigerian Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_Americans) and [Ethiopian Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_Americans) were the most reported sub-Saharan African groups in the United States.[^cite_note-108]&#x20;

In the 2020 census, the African American population was undercounted at an estimated rate of 3.3%, up from 2.1% in 2010.[^cite_note-109]&#x20;

[Texas](29810.md) has the largest African American population by state with approximately four million.[^cite_note-WPR-110] Following Texas is [Florida](18933066.md), with 3.8 million, and [Georgia](48830.md), with 3.6 million.[^cite_note-111] [Mississippi](16949861.md) is the state with the highest African American share of the population at 39%. Following Mississippi is [Louisiana](18130.md) at 34%, and Georgia at 32%.[^cite_note-WPR-110]&#x20;

### US cities

Further information: [List of U.S. cities with large Black populations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._cities_with_large_Black_populations) and [List of U.S. metropolitan areas with large African-American populations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._metropolitan_areas_with_large_African-American_populations)

After 100 years of African Americans leaving the south in large numbers seeking better opportunities and treatment in the west and north, a movement known as the [Great Migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Migration_\(African_American\)), there is now a reverse trend, called the [New Great Migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Great_Migration). As with the earlier Great Migration, the New Great Migration is primarily directed toward cities and large urban areas, such as [Charlotte](57447.md), [Houston](13774.md), [Dallas](53838.md), [Fort Worth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Worth), [Huntsville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huntsville,_Alabama), [Raleigh](57845.md), [Tampa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tampa), [San Antonio](53848.md), [New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans,_Louisiana), [Memphis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memphis,_Tennessee), [Nashville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nashville), [Jacksonville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacksonville), and so forth.[^cite_note-auto1-112] A growing percentage of African Americans from the west and north are migrating to the southern region of the US for economic and cultural reasons. In 2020, [New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_metropolitan_area), [Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_metropolitan_area), and [Los Angeles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Los_Angeles) metropolitan areas had the highest decline in African Americans, while [Atlanta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_Atlanta), [Dallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas%E2%80%93Fort_Worth_metroplex), and [Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Houston) had the highest increase respectively.[^cite_note-113][^cite_note-114] Despite recent declines, as of 2020, the [New York City metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_City_metropolitan_area) still has the largest African American metropolitan population in the United States and the only to have over three million African Americans.[^cite_note-115][^cite_note-116]&#x20;

Among [cities of 100,000 or more](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._cities_with_large_Black_populations), [South Fulton, Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Fulton,_Georgia) had the highest percentage of Black residents of any large US city in 2020, with 93%. Other large cities with African American majorities include [Jackson, Mississippi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackson,_Mississippi) (80%), [Detroit, Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit,_Michigan) (80%), [Birmingham, Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham,_Alabama) (70%), [Miami Gardens, Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_Gardens,_Florida) (67%), [Memphis, Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memphis,_Tennessee) (63%), [Montgomery, Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montgomery,_Alabama) (62%), [Baltimore, Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltimore) (60%), [Augusta, Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augusta,_Georgia) (59%), [Shreveport, Louisiana](150473.md) (58%), [New Orleans, Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans,_Louisiana) (57%), [Macon, Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macon,_Georgia) (56%), [Baton Rouge, Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baton_Rouge,_Louisiana) (55%), [Hampton, Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hampton,_Virginia) (53%), [Newark, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newark,_New_Jersey) (53%), [Mobile, Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobile,_Alabama) (53%), [Cleveland, Ohio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleveland,_Ohio) (52%), and [Brockton, Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brockton,_Massachusetts) (51%).&#x20;

[Claiborne County, Mississippi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claiborne_County,_Mississippi) is the Blackest [county](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County) in the U.S. at 87% Black in 2020. [Cook County, Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cook_County,_Illinois) has the largest Black population in the U.S. with 1,185,601 Black residents in 2020.&#x20;

The nation's most affluent community with an African American majority resides in [View Park–Windsor Hills, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/View_Park%E2%80%93Windsor_Hills,_California), with an annual median household income of \$159,618 and median home price of about \$1.5 million in 2025.[^cite_note-117][^cite_note-118] Other largely affluent and African American communities include [Prince George's County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_George%27s_County,_Maryland) (namely [Mitchellville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitchellville,_Maryland), [Woodmore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodmore,_Maryland), [Upper Marlboro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Marlboro,_Maryland)) and [Charles County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_County,_Maryland) in Maryland,[^cite_note-119] [DeKalb County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DeKalb_County,_Georgia) (namely [Stonecrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonecrest,_Georgia), [Lithonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithonia,_Georgia), [Smoke Rise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smoke_Rise,_Georgia)) and South Fulton in Georgia, [Charles City County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_City_County,_Virginia) in Virginia, [Baldwin Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baldwin_Hills,_Los_Angeles) in California, [Hillcrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillcrest,_Rockland_County,_New_York) and [Uniondale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uniondale,_New_York) in New York, and [Cedar Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cedar_Hill,_Texas), [DeSoto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DeSoto,_Texas), and [Missouri City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri_City,_Texas) in Texas. Additionally, there is a significant affluent Black presence in the southern Chicago suburbs of [Cook County, Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cook_County,_Illinois). A report from the National Association of Real Estate Brokers (NAREB) indicated that five of the top ten municipalities nationwide (with at least 500 Black households) registering the highest Black homeownership rates were in this area – including [Olympia Fields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympia_Fields,_Illinois), [South Holland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Holland,_Illinois), [Flossmoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flossmoor,_Illinois), [Matteson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matteson,_Illinois), and [Lynwood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynwood,_Illinois).[^cite_note-120] In 2006, [Queens County, New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queens) was the only county with a population of 65,000 or more where African Americans had a higher median household income than White Americans.[^cite_note-Queens-121]&#x20;

[Seatack, Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seatack,_Virginia) is currently the oldest African American community in the United States.[^cite_note-122] It survives today with a vibrant and active civic community.[^cite_note-123]&#x20;

### Education

Main article: [History of African-American education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_African-American_education)



Former slave learning to read, 1870

During slavery, [anti-literacy laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-literacy_laws_in_the_United_States) were enacted in the US that prohibited education for Black people. Slave owners saw literacy as a threat to the institution of slavery. As a North Carolina statute stated, "Teaching slaves to read and write, tends to excite dissatisfaction in their minds, and to produce insurrection and [rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_rebellion)."[^cite_note-124]&#x20;

When slavery was finally abolished in 1865, public educational systems were expanding across the country. By 1870, around seventy-four institutions in the south provided a form of advanced education for African American students. By 1900, over a hundred programs at these schools provided training for Black professionals, including teachers. Many of the students at Fisk University, including the young [W. E. B. Du Bois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W._E._B._Du_Bois), taught school during the summers to support their studies.[^cite_note-125]&#x20;

African Americans were very concerned to provide quality education for their children, but White supremacy limited their ability to participate in educational policymaking on the political level. State governments soon moved to undermine their citizenship by restricting their right to vote. By the late 1870s, Blacks were disenfranchised and segregated across the American South.[^cite_note-126] White politicians in Mississippi and other states withheld financial resources and supplies from Black schools. Nevertheless, the presence of Black teachers, and their engagement with their communities both inside and outside the classroom, ensured that Black students had access to education despite these external constraints.[^cite_note-127][^cite_note-128]&#x20;



Students in the one-room Waldorf Negro elementary school in [Waldorf, Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waldorf,_Maryland), 1941

During [World War II](32927.md), demands for unity and racial tolerance on the home front provided an opening for the first Black history curriculum in the country.[^cite_note-129] For example, during the early 1940s, Madeline Morgan, a Black teacher in the Chicago public schools, created a curriculum for students in grades one through eight highlighting the contributions of Black people to the history of the United States. At the close of the war, Chicago's Board of Education downgraded the curriculum's status from mandatory to optional.[^cite_note-130]&#x20;

Predominantly Black schools for kindergarten through twelfth grade students were common throughout the US before the 1970s. By 1972, however, desegregation efforts meant that only 25% of Black students were in schools with more than 90% non-White students. However, since then, a trend towards re-segregation affected communities across the country: by 2011, 2.9 million African American students were in such overwhelmingly minority schools, including 53% of Black students in school districts that were formerly under desegregation orders.[^cite_note-131][^cite_note-132]&#x20;

As late as 1947, about one third of African Americans over 65 were considered to lack the literacy to read and write their own names. By 1969, [illiteracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illiteracy) as it had been traditionally defined, had been largely eradicated among younger African Americans.[^cite_note-133]&#x20;



Astrophysicist [Neil deGrasse Tyson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_deGrasse_Tyson) is director of New York City's [Hayden Planetarium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hayden_Planetarium).

Between 1995 and 2009, freshmen college enrollment for African Americans increased by 73 percent and only 15 percent for Whites.[^cite_note-134] Black women are enrolled in college more than any other race and gender group, leading all with 9.7% enrolled according to the 2011 US census.[^cite_note-135][^cite_note-136] The average high school graduation rate of Blacks in the United States has steadily increased to 71% in 2013.[^cite_note-137] Separating this statistic into component parts shows it varies greatly depending upon the state and the school district examined. 38% of Black males graduated in the state of New York but in Maine 97% graduated and exceeded the White male graduation rate by 11 percentage points.[^cite_note-138] In much of the southeastern United States and some parts of the southwestern United States the graduation rate of White males was in fact below 70% such as in Florida where 62% of White males graduated from high school. Examining specific school districts paints an even more complex picture. In the Detroit school district, the graduation rate of Black males was 20% but 7% for White males. In the New York City school district 28% of Black males graduate from high school compared to 57% of White males. In Newark County 76% of Black males graduated compared to 67% for White males. Further academic improvement has occurred in 2015. Roughly 23% of all Blacks have bachelor's degrees. In 1988, 21% of Whites had obtained a bachelor's degree versus 11% of Blacks. In 2015, 23% of Blacks had obtained a bachelor's degree versus 36% of Whites.[^cite_note-Census_Report-139] Foreign born Blacks, 9% of the Black population, made even greater strides. They exceed native born Blacks by 10 percentage points.[^cite_note-Census_Report-139]&#x20;

[College Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_Board), which runs the official college-level [advanced placement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advanced_Placement) (AP) programs in American high schools, have has received criticism in recent years that its curricula have focused too much on [Euro-centric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euro-centric) history.[^cite_note-Columbia-140] In 2020, College Board reshaped some curricula among history-based courses to further reflect the [African diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_diaspora).[^cite_note-141] In 2021, College Board announced it would be piloting an [AP African American Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AP_African_American_Studies) course between 2022 and 2024. The course officially launched in August 2024.[^cite_note-TIG-142][^cite_note-143]&#x20;

In June 2023, the [Supreme Court ended race-based affirmative action at American colleges and universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Students_for_Fair_Admissions_v._Harvard). This landmark Supreme Court decision is widely believed to contribute to a decline in African American enrollment at the nation's most selective and prominent colleges and universities, where African American applicants often have, on average, lower standardized test scores and GPAs compared to the overall applicant pool. In response, many of the nation's most prominent [historically black colleges and universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historically_black_colleges_and_universities) (HBCUs) have reported a significant surge in applications and enrollment.[^cite_note-144][^cite_note-145][^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147]&#x20;

Slightly more than 250,000 African American students in the high school graduating class of 2025 took the [Scholastic Aptitude Test (SAT)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scholastic_Aptitude_Test) making up 13% of test takers. African American students had an average score of 904 which is lower than the national average of 1,209. Asian American students had the highest average with 1,229.[^cite_note-148]&#x20;

According to a 2025 study, African Americans have the highest average [student debt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_loans_in_the_United_States). African Americans with [bachelor's degrees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bachelor%27s_degrees) owe an average of \$52,726 in student loans. Nearly 70% of African Americans took out a loan to fund their undergraduate education.[^cite_note-149] African American women, in particular, carry the highest average student debt in the nation.[^cite_note-150]&#x20;

#### Historically black colleges and universities

Main articles: [Historically black colleges and universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historically_black_colleges_and_universities), [List of historically black colleges and universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_historically_black_colleges_and_universities), and [African American student access to medical schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_American_student_access_to_medical_schools)

Historically black colleges and universities (HBCUs), which were founded when [segregated institutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation_in_the_United_States) of higher learning did not admit African Americans, continue to thrive and educate students of all races today. There are 107 HBCUs representing three percent of the nation's colleges and universities with the majority established in the [Southeast](816925.md).[^cite_note-151][^cite_note-152] HBCUs have been largely responsible for establishing and expanding the African American middle-class by providing more career opportunities for African Americans.[^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154]&#x20;

### Economic status

Further information: [Black-owned business](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black-owned_business)

The economic disparity between the races in the US has marginally improved since the end of slavery. In 1863, two years prior to emancipation, Black people owned 0.5 percent of the national wealth, while in 2019 it is just over 1.5 percent.[^cite_note-155] [Racial disparity in poverty rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_inequality_in_the_United_States) has narrowed since the civil rights era, with the [poverty rate among African Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_poverty) decreasing from 24.7% in 2004 to 18.8% in 2020, compared to 10.5% for all Americans.[^cite_note-DeNavas-Walt-156][^cite_note-157] Poverty is associated with higher rates of marital stress and dissolution, [physical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physical_disorder) and [mental health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mental_disorder) problems, [disability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disability_and_poverty), [cognitive deficits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_deficit), [low educational attainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achievement_gap_in_the_United_States), and crime.[^cite_note-CharacOfFam-158]&#x20;

African Americans have a long and diverse history of business ownership. Although the first African American business is unknown, slaves captured from West Africa are believed to have established commercial enterprises as peddlers and skilled craftspeople as far back as the 17th century. Around 1900, Booker T. Washington became the most famous proponent of African American businesses. His critic and rival W. E. B. DuBois also commended business as a vehicle for African American advancement.[^cite_note-159]&#x20;



This graph shows the real median [US household income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Household_income_in_the_United_States) by race: 1967 to 2011, in 2011 dollars.

[^cite_note-160]

African Americans had a combined buying power of over \$1.6 trillion as of 2021, a 171% increase of their buying power in 2000 but lagging significantly in growth behind American [Latinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans) and [Asians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Americans) in the same timer period (with 288% and 383%, respectively; for reference, US growth overall was 144% in the same period); however, African American net worth had shrunk 14% in the previous year despite strong growth in property prices and the [S\&P 500](170992.md). In 2002, African American-owned businesses accounted for 1.2 million of the US's 23 million businesses.[^cite_note-161] As of 2011, African American-owned businesses account for approximately two million [US businesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_businesses).[^cite_note-Tozzi-162] Black-owned businesses experienced the largest growth in number of businesses among minorities from 2002 to 2011.[^cite_note-Tozzi-162]&#x20;

Twenty-five percent of Blacks had [white-collar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White-collar_worker) occupations (management, professional, and related fields) in 2000, compared with 33.6% of Americans overall.[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-Black_Pop-March_2002-164] In 2001, over half of African American households of married couples earned \$50,000 or more.[^cite_note-Black_Pop-March_2002-164] Although in the same year African Americans were over-represented among the nation's poor, this was directly related to the disproportionate percentage of African American families headed by single women; such families are collectively poorer, regardless of ethnicity.[^cite_note-Black_Pop-March_2002-164]&#x20;

In 2006, the median earnings of African American men was more than Black and non-Black American women overall, and in all educational levels.[^cite_note-census.gov-PINC03-165][^cite_note-census.gov-254-166][^cite_note-census.gov-259-167][^cite_note-census.gov-135-168][^cite_note-169] At the same time, among American men, income disparities were significant; the median income of African American men was approximately 76 cents for every dollar of their European American counterparts, although the gap narrowed somewhat with a rise in educational level.[^cite_note-census.gov-PINC03-165][^cite_note-170]&#x20;

Overall, the median earnings of African American men were 72 cents for every dollar earned of their Asian American counterparts, and \$1.17 for every dollar earned by Hispanic men.[^cite_note-census.gov-PINC03-165][^cite_note-census.gov-135-168][^cite_note-171] On the other hand, by 2006, among American women with post-secondary education, African American women have made significant advances; the median income of African American women was more than those of their Asian-, European- and Hispanic American counterparts with at least some college education.[^cite_note-census.gov-254-166][^cite_note-census.gov-259-167][^cite_note-172]&#x20;

The US [public sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_sector) is the single most important source of employment for African Americans.[^cite_note-laborcenter.berkeley.edu-173] During 2008–2010, 21.2% of all Black workers were public employees, compared with 16.3% of non-Black workers.[^cite_note-laborcenter.berkeley.edu-173] Both before and after the onset of the [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession), African Americans were 30% more likely than other workers to be employed in the public sector.[^cite_note-laborcenter.berkeley.edu-173] The public sector is also a critical source of decent-paying jobs for Black Americans. For both men and women, the median wage earned by Black employees is significantly higher in the public sector than in other industries.[^cite_note-laborcenter.berkeley.edu-173]&#x20;

In 1999, the median income of African American families was \$33,255 compared to \$53,356 of European Americans. In times of economic hardship for the nation, African Americans suffer disproportionately from job loss and [underemployment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Underemployment), with the Black underclass being hardest hit. The phrase "last hired and first fired" is reflected in the [Bureau of Labor Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau_of_Labor_Statistics) unemployment figures. Nationwide, the October 2008 unemployment rate for African Americans was 11.1%,[^cite_note-174] while the nationwide rate was 6.5%.[^cite_note-175] In 2007, the average income for African Americans was approximately \$34,000, compared to \$55,000 for Whites.[^cite_note-176] African Americans experience a higher rate of unemployment than the general population.[^cite_note-177]&#x20;

The income gap between Black and White families is also significant. In 2005, employed Blacks earned 65% of the wages of Whites, down from 82% in 1975.[^cite_note-DeNavas-Walt-156] *The New York Times* reported in 2006 that in [Queens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queens), New York, the median income among African American families exceeded that of White families, which the newspaper attributed to the growth in the number of two-parent Black families. It noted that Queens was the only county with more than 65,000 residents where that was true.[^cite_note-Queens-121] In 2011, it was reported that [72% of Black babies were born to unwed mothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_family_structure).[^cite_note-178] The poverty rate among single-parent Black families was 39.5% in 2005, according to [Walter E. Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_E._Williams), while it was 9.9% among married-couple Black families. Among White families, the respective rates were 26.4% and 6% in poverty.[^cite_note-179]&#x20;

Collectively, African Americans are more involved in the American political process than other minority groups in the United States, indicated by the highest level of voter registration and participation in elections among these groups in 2004.[^cite_note-vote-nov2007-180] African Americans also have the highest level of [Congressional representation](31756.md) of any minority group in the US.[^cite_note-181]&#x20;

#### African American homeownership



The [US homeownership rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeownership_in_the_United_States) according to race

[^cite_note-US_Census_Bureau,_homeownership_by_race-182]

[Homeownership in the US](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home-ownership_in_the_United_States) is the strongest indicator of financial stability and the primary asset most Americans use to generate wealth. African Americans continue to lag behind other racial groups in homeownership.[^cite_note-183] In the first quarter of 2021, 45.1% of African Americans owned their homes, compared to 65.3% of all Americans.[^cite_note-184] The African American homeownership rate has remained relatively flat since the 1970s despite an increase in [anti-discrimination housing laws and protections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Housing_discrimination_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-185] The African American homeownership rate peaked in 2004 at 49.7%.[^cite_note-186]&#x20;

The average White high school drop-out still has a slightly better chance of owning a home than the average African American college graduate usually due to unfavorable [debt-to-income ratios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debt-to-income_ratio) or [credit scores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Credit_score_in_the_United_States) among most African American college graduates.[^cite_note-187][^cite_note-188] Since 2000, fast-growing housing costs in most cities have made it even more difficult for the US African American homeownership rate to significantly grow and reach over 50% for the first time in history. From 2000 to 2022, the median home price in the US grew 160%, outpacing average annual household income growth in that same period, which only grew about 30%.[^cite_note-189][^cite_note-190][^cite_note-191] [South Carolina](27956.md) is the state with the most African American homeownership, with about 55% of African Americans owning their own homes.[^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193]&#x20;
