Black people, who make up 12% of the total U.S. population, make up 32% of all people experiencing homelessness, according to the data.[^cite_note-194]&#x20;

### Politics

| Year | Candidate of the plurality | Political  party | % of Black vote | Result |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1980 | [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter) | [Democratic](5043544.md) | 83% | Lost |
| 1984 | [Walter Mondale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Mondale) | Democratic | 91% | Lost |
| 1988 | [Michael Dukakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Dukakis) | Democratic | 89% | Lost |
| 1992 | [Bill Clinton](3356.md) | Democratic | 83% | Won |
| 1996 | [Bill Clinton](3356.md) | Democratic | 84% | Won |
| 2000 | [Al Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Gore) | Democratic | 90% | Lost |
| 2004 | [John Kerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerry) | Democratic | 88% | Lost |
| 2008 | [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) | Democratic | 95% | Won |
| 2012 | [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) | Democratic | 93% | Won |
| 2016 | [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton) | Democratic | 88% | Lost |
| 2020 | [Joe Biden](145422.md) | Democratic | 87% | Won |
| 2024 | [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) | Democratic | 85% | Lost |

Since the mid 20th century, a large majority of African Americans support the [Democratic Party](5043544.md). In the [2024 Presidential election](48410011.md), 86% of African American voters supported Democrat [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris), while 13% supported Republican [Donald Trump](4848272.md).[^cite_note-195] Although there is an African American lobby in foreign policy, it has not had the impact that African American organizations have had in domestic policy.[^cite_note-196]&#x20;

Many African Americans were excluded from electoral politics in the decades following the end of Reconstruction. For those that could participate, until the [New Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Deal), African Americans were supporters of the Republican Party because it was Republican President Abraham Lincoln who helped in granting freedom to American slaves; at the time, the Republicans and Democrats represented the [sectional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sectionalism) interests of the [North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_United_States) and [South](179553.md), respectively, rather than any specific ideology, and both [conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States) and [liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_liberalism_in_the_United_States) were represented equally in both parties.&#x20;

The African American trend of voting for Democrats can be traced back to the 1930s during the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression), when [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt)'s [New Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Deal) program provided economic relief to African Americans. Roosevelt's [New Deal coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Deal_coalition) turned the Democratic Party into an organization of the working class and their liberal allies, regardless of region. The African American vote became even more solidly Democratic when Democratic presidents [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy) and [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson) pushed for civil rights legislation during the 1960s. In 1960, nearly a third of African Americans voted for Republican [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon).[^cite_note-197]&#x20;

[Conservatism](6675.md) has been growing among African Americans, particularly since the 2020 Presidential election. In the 2024 election, Trump secured a slightly larger share of the African American vote compared to his 2020 performance.[^cite_note-198][^cite_note-199][^cite_note-200]&#x20;

#### Black national anthem



&#x20;"[Lift Every Voice and Sing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lift_Every_Voice_and_Sing)" being sung by the [family of Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_of_Barack_Obama), [Smokey Robinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smokey_Robinson) and others in the [White House](33057.md) in 2014

"[Lift Every Voice and Sing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lift_Every_Voice_and_Sing)" is often referred to as the Black national anthem in the United States.[^cite_note-201] In 1919, the National Association for the Advancement of Colored People (NAACP) had dubbed it the "Negro national anthem" for its power in voicing a cry for liberation and affirmation for African-American people.[^cite_note-202]&#x20;

### Religion

Religious affiliation of African Americans in 2007

[^cite_note-203]

1. [Black Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Protestant) (59.0%)

2. [Evangelical Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelical_Protestant) (15.0%)

3. [Mainline Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainline_Protestant) (4.00%)

4. [Roman Catholic](606848.md) (5.00%)

5. [Jehovah's Witness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah%27s_Witness) (1.00%)

6. Other Christian (1.00%)

7. Muslim (1.00%)

8. Other religion (1.00%)

9. Unaffiliated (11.0%)

10. Atheist or agnostic (2.00%)

Main article: [Religion of Black Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_of_Black_Americans)

Further information: [Black church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_church), [Hoodoo (folk magic)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoodoo_\(folk_magic\)), and [Louisiana Voodoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Voodoo)



[Mount Zion United Methodist Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Zion_United_Methodist_Church_\(Washington,_D.C.\)) is the oldest African American congregation in Washington, D.C.



[Masjid Malcolm Shabazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masjid_Malcolm_Shabazz) in Harlem, New York City

The majority of African Americans are [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant), many of whom follow the historically Black churches.[^cite_note-PewForum-204] The term [Black church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_church) refers to churches which minister to predominantly African American congregations. Black congregations were first established by freed slaves at the end of the 17th century, and later when slavery was abolished more African Americans were allowed to create a unique form of Christianity that was culturally influenced by African spiritual traditions.[^cite_note-205] One of these early African American Christian cultural traditions in the Black Church is the [Watchnight service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watchnight_service), also called Freedom's Eve, where African American congregations all over the nation come together on New Year's Eve through New Year's morning in remembrance of the eve and New Year of their emancipation, sharing testimonies, being baptized and partaking in praise and worship.[^cite_note-206]&#x20;

According to a 2007 survey, more than half of the African American population are part of the historically Black churches.[^cite_note-religions-207] The largest Protestant denomination among African Americans is the [Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptists),[^cite_note-208] distributed mainly in four denominations, the largest being the [National Baptist Convention, USA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Baptist_Convention,_USA,_Inc.) and the [National Baptist Convention of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Baptist_Convention_of_America,_Inc.).[^cite_note-church-209] The second-largest is the [Methodists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodist),[^cite_note-doindrugs-210] the largest organizations of which are the [African Methodist Episcopal Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Methodist_Episcopal_Church) and the [African Methodist Episcopal Zion Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Methodist_Episcopal_Zion_Church).[^cite_note-church-209][^cite_note-211]&#x20;

[Pentecostals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostalism) are distributed among several different religious bodies, with the [Church of God in Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_God_in_Christ) as the largest among them by far.[^cite_note-church-209] About 16% of African American Christians are members of White Protestant communions;[^cite_note-doindrugs-210] these denominations (which include the [United Church of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Church_of_Christ)) mostly have a 2–3% African American membership.[^cite_note-212] There are also large numbers of [Catholics](606848.md), constituting 5% of the African American population.[^cite_note-religions-207] Of the total number of [Jehovah's Witnesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah%27s_Witnesses), 22% are Black.[^cite_note-PewForum-204]&#x20;

Some African Americans follow [Islam](6037917.md). Historically, between 15 and 30% of enslaved Africans brought to the Americas were [Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim), but most of these Africans were converted to Christianity during the era of American slavery.[^cite_note-213] During the twentieth century, some African Americans converted to Islam, mainly through the influence of [Black nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_nationalism) groups that preached with distinctive Islamic practices; including the [Moorish Science Temple of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moorish_Science_Temple_of_America), and the largest organization, the [Nation of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation_of_Islam), founded in the 1930s, which attracted at least 20,000 people by 1963.[^cite_note-214][^cite_note-215] Prominent members included activist [Malcolm X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malcolm_X) and boxer [Muhammad Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Ali).[^cite_note-216]&#x20;



[Muhammad Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Ali) converted to Islam in 1964.

Malcolm X is considered the first person to start the movement among African Americans towards mainstream Islam, after he left the Nation and made the [pilgrimage to Mecca](23487999.md).[^cite_note-217] In 1975, [Warith Deen Mohammed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warith_Deen_Mohammed), the son of [Elijah Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elijah_Muhammad) took control of the Nation after his father's death and guided the majority of its members to [orthodox Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam).[^cite_note-218]&#x20;

[African American Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_Muslims) constitute 20% of the total [US Muslim population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_the_United_States),[^cite_note-PewMuslim-219] the majority are [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam) or orthodox Muslims, some of these identify under the community of [W. Deen Mohammed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W._Deen_Mohammed).[^cite_note-220][^cite_note-221] The Nation of Islam led by [Louis Farrakhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Farrakhan) has a membership ranging from 20,000 to 50,000 members.[^cite_note-222]&#x20;

There is also a small but growing group of [African American Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_Jews), making up less than 0.5% of African Americans or about 2% of the [Jewish population in the United States](1131183.md). The majority of African-American Jews are [Ashkenazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi_Jews), while smaller numbers identify as [Sephardi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardic_Jews), [Mizrahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizrahi_Jews), or other.[^cite_note-\:0a-223][^cite_note-224][^cite_note-225] Many African-American Jews are affiliated with denominations such as the [Reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Judaism), [Conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Judaism), [Reconstructionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstructionist_Judaism), or [Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Judaism) branches of Judaism, but the majority identify as "Jews of no religion", commonly known as secular Jews. A significant number of people who identify themselves as "Black Jews" are affiliated with [syncretic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_syncretism) religious groups, largely the [Black Hebrew Israelites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Hebrew_Israelites), whose beliefs include the claim that African Americans are descended from the Biblical [Israelites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israelites).[^cite_note-northstar-226] Jews of all races typically do not accept Black Hebrew Israelites as Jews, in part because they are usually not Jewish [according to Jewish law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Who_is_a_Jew),[^cite_note-227] and in part because these groups are sometimes associated with antisemitism.[^cite_note-228][^cite_note-229] African-American Jews have criticized the Black Hebrew Israelites, regarding the movement as primarily composed of Black non-Jews who have appropriated Black-Jewish identity.[^cite_note-230]&#x20;

In the 2010s, confirmed [atheists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheism) were less than one half of one percent, similar to numbers for [Hispanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic).[^cite_note-231][^cite_note-232][^cite_note-233]&#x20;

In 2025, a [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) study found that, among Black Americans, Christian affiliation has declined in recent years. The share of Black adults identifying as Christian fell from 85% in 2007 to 79% in 2014 and 73% in 2021, mirroring a broader decline in Christianity in the United States. However, Black adults remain more likely than U.S. adults overall to identify as Christian (73% vs. 62%). About 4% of Black adults identify with non-Christian religions, mostly Islam, while 22% are religiously unaffiliated ("nones"), including 3% atheist or agnostic and 19% identifying as "nothing in particular." 58% say religion is very important in their lives, 64% pray daily, and 57% attend religious services at least monthly, whether in person or online. By comparison, about half or fewer adults in other racial or ethnic groups report that religion is very important or that they pray daily, and even smaller shares attend services monthly. Religiosity among Black Americans also varies by age. 39% of Black adults aged 65 and older describe themselves as very religious, compared with 21% of those under 30. This age pattern is similar to that observed among White and Hispanic Americans, although Black adults of all ages are more likely than other groups to describe themselves as very religious.[^cite_note-auto3-3]&#x20;

### Sexuality

See also: [African-American LGBT community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_LGBT_community)

According to a [Gallup survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup_survey), 4.6% of Black or African Americans self-identified as [LGBT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_the_United_States) in 2016,[^cite_note-More_Adults-234] while the total portion of American adults in all ethnic groups identifying as LGBT was 4.1% in 2016.[^cite_note-More_Adults-234] African American women are more likely than African American men to openly identify as LGBT.[^cite_note-235]&#x20;

### Health

Further information: [Race and health in the United States § African Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_and_health_in_the_United_States#African_Americans)

See also: [Impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on black people § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_black_people#United_States)

#### General health

See also: [Alzheimer's disease in African Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alzheimer%27s_disease_in_African_Americans)

The life expectancy for Black men in 2008 was 70.8 years.[^cite_note-Los_Angeles_Times-236] Life expectancy for Black women was 77.5 years in 2008.[^cite_note-Los_Angeles_Times-236] In 1900, when information on Black life expectancy started being collated, a Black man could expect to live to 32.5 years and a Black woman 33.5 years.[^cite_note-Los_Angeles_Times-236] In 1900, White men lived an average of 46.3 years and White women lived an average of 48.3 years.[^cite_note-Los_Angeles_Times-236] African American life expectancy at birth is persistently five to seven years lower than [European Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Americans).[^cite_note-lavest-237] Black men have shorter lifespans than any other group in the US besides Native American men.[^cite_note-Gilbert-238]&#x20;

Black people have higher rates of [obesity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obesity), [diabetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diabetes), and [hypertension](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypertension) than the US average.[^cite_note-Los_Angeles_Times-236] For adult Black men, the rate of obesity was 31.6% in 2010.[^cite_note-cdc.gov-239] For adult Black women, the rate of obesity was 41.2% in 2010.[^cite_note-cdc.gov-239] African Americans have higher rates of mortality than any other racial or ethnic group for eight of the top ten causes of death.[^cite_note-hummer2004-240] In 2013, among men, Black men had the highest rate of getting cancer, followed by White, Hispanic, Asian/Pacific Islander (A/PI), and American Indian/Alaska Native (AI/AN) men. Among women, White women had the highest rate of getting cancer, followed by Black, Hispanic, Asian/Pacific Islander, and American Indian/Alaska Native women.[^cite_note-241] African Americans also have higher prevalence and incidence of Alzheimer's disease compared to the overall average.[^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243]&#x20;

Black women lead the nation in [abortions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortions). According to a 2022 report, Black women made up 40% of abortions despite making up 13% of the U.S. woman population.[^cite_note-244][^cite_note-245] African Americans are more likely than White Americans to die due to health-related problems developed by [alcoholism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcoholism). Alcohol abuse is the main contributor to the top three causes of death among African Americans.[^cite_note-246]&#x20;

In December 2020, African Americans were less likely to be [vaccinated](63319438.md) against [COVID-19](63030231.md) due to mistrust in the US medical system. From 2021 to 2022, there was an increase in African Americans who became vaccinated.[^cite_note-247][^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249] Still, in 2022, COVID-19 complications became the third leading cause of death for African Americans.[^cite_note-250]&#x20;

Violence is a major problem within the African American community.[^cite_note-lacasa.org-251][^cite_note-252] A report from the [US Department of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Department_of_Justice) states "In 2005, homicide victimization rates for Blacks were more than six times as high as the rates for Whites".[^cite_note-Homicide_trends_in_the_US-253] The report also found that "94% of Black victims were killed by Blacks."[^cite_note-Homicide_trends_in_the_US-253] Of the nearly 20,000 recorded US homicides in 2022, African Americans made up the majority of offenders and victims despite making up less than 15% of the population.[^cite_note-254] In 2024, all of the five most dangerous US cities have a significant Black population and highly concerning Black-on-Black violent crime rate.[^cite_note-255] Black males age 15–44 are the only race/sex category for which homicide is a top-five cause of death.[^cite_note-Gilbert-238] Black women are three times more likely to be killed by an intimate partner than White women.[^cite_note-lacasa.org-251] Black children are more than three times as likely to die due to parental abuse and neglect than White children.[^cite_note-256]&#x20;

#### Sexual health

According to the [Centers for Disease Control and Prevention](6811.md), African Americans have higher rates of [sexually transmitted infections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexually_transmitted_infections) (STIs) compared to Whites, with five times the rates of [syphilis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syphilis) and [chlamydia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chlamydia_infection), and 7.5 times the rate of [gonorrhea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gonorrhea).[^cite_note-257]&#x20;

The disproportionately high incidence of [HIV/AIDS among African Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV/AIDS_in_the_United_States) has been attributed to [homophobic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homophobia_in_the_African_American_community) influences, lack of [condom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Condom) usage, and lack of proper healthcare.[^cite_note-258] The prevalence of [HIV/AIDS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV/AIDS) among Black men is seven times higher than the prevalence for White men, and Black men are more than nine times as likely to die from HIV/AIDS-related illness than White men.[^cite_note-Gilbert-238] The prevalence of HIV/AIDS among Black women is 20 times higher than White women, and Black women are more than 15 times as likely to die from HIV/AIDS-related illness than White women.[^cite_note-259][^cite_note-260]&#x20;

#### Mental health

African Americans have several [barriers for accessing mental health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obstacles_to_receiving_mental_health_services_among_African_American_youth) services. [Counseling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mental_health_counselor) has been frowned upon and distant in utility and proximity to many people in the African American community. In 2004, a qualitative research study explored the disconnect with African Americans and mental health. The study was conducted as a semi-structured discussion which allowed the focus group to express their opinions and life experiences. The results revealed a couple key variables that create barriers for many African American communities to seek mental health services such as the stigma, lack of four important necessities; trust, affordability, cultural understanding and impersonal services.[^cite_note-\:0-261]&#x20;

Historically, many African American communities did not seek counseling because religion was a part of the family values.[^cite_note-262] African Americans who have a faith background are more likely to seek prayer as a coping mechanism for mental issues rather than seeking professional mental health services.[^cite_note-\:0-261] In 2015 a study concluded, African Americans with high value in religion are less likely to utilize mental health services compared to those who have low value in religion.[^cite_note-263]&#x20;

In the United States, counseling approaches are based on the experience of [White Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Americans) and do not fit within the African American culture. African American families tend to resolve concerns within the family, and it is viewed by the family as a strength. On the other hand, when African Americans seek counseling, they face a social backlash and are criticized. They may be labeled "crazy", viewed as weak, and their pride is diminished.[^cite_note-\:0-261] Because of this, many African Americans instead seek mentorship within communities they trust.&#x20;

Terminology is another barrier in relation to African Americans and mental health. There is more stigma on the term *[psychotherapy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychotherapy)* versus counseling. In one study, psychotherapy is associated with mental illness whereas counseling approaches problem-solving, guidance and help.[^cite_note-\:0-261] More African Americans seek assistance when it is called counseling and not psychotherapy because it is more welcoming within the culture and community.[^cite_note-264] Counselors are encouraged to be aware of such barriers for the well-being of African American clients. Without [cultural competency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_competence_in_healthcare) training in health care, many African Americans go unheard and misunderstood.[^cite_note-\:0-261]&#x20;

In 2021, African Americans had the third highest [suicide](16831059.md) rate trailing American Indians/Alaska Natives and White Americans. However, African Americans had the second highest increase of its suicide rate from 2011 to 2021, growing 58%.[^cite_note-265] As of 2024, suicide is the second leading cause of death among African-Americans between the ages of 15 and 24, with Black men being four times more likely to kill themselves than Black women.[^cite_note-266]&#x20;

## Genetics

See also: [Genetic history of the African diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_history_of_the_African_diaspora)

### Genome-wide studies



Genetic clustering of 128 African Americans, by Zakharia et al. (2009). Each vertical bar represents an individual. The color scheme of the bar plot matches that in the PCA plot.

[^cite_note-Zakharia2009-267]

Recent studies of African Americans using genetic testing have found ancestry to vary by region and sex of ancestors. These studies found that on average, African Americans have 73.2–82.1% Sub-Saharan African, 16.7–24% European, and 0.8–1.2% Native American genetic ancestry, with large variation between individuals.[^cite_note-Bryc2009-268][^cite_note-Bryc_2015-269][^cite_note-270] Commercial testing services have reported similar variation, with ranges from 0.6 to 2 percent Native American, 19 to 29 percent European, and 65 to 80 percent Sub-Saharan African ancestry.[^cite_note-271]&#x20;

According to a genome-wide study by Bryc et al. (2009), the mixed ancestry of African Americans in varying ratios came about as the result of sexual contact between West/Central Africans (more frequently females) and Europeans (more frequently males). This can be understood as most commonly being the result of enslaved African American females being [raped](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_sexual_slavery_in_the_United_States#Under_chattel_slavery) by White males.[^cite_note-272] Consequently, the 365 African Americans in their sample have a genome-wide average of 78.1% West African ancestry and 18.5% European ancestry, with large variation among individuals (ranging from 99% to 1% West African ancestry). The West African ancestral component in African Americans is most similar to that in present-day speakers from the non-[Bantu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bantu_languages) branches of the [Niger-Congo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niger-Congo_languages) family.[^cite_note-Bryc2009-268][^cite_note-274]&#x20;

Correspondingly, Montinaro et al. (2014) observed that around 50% of the overall ancestry of African Americans traces comes from a population similar to the Niger-Congo-speaking [Yoruba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_people) of southern [Nigeria](21383.md) and southern [Benin](3459.md), reflecting the centrality of this West African region in the Atlantic slave trade. The next most frequent ancestral component found among African Americans was derived from Great Britain, in keeping with historical records. It constitutes a little over 10% of their overall ancestry and is most similar to the Northwest European ancestral component also carried by [Barbadians](6960207.md).[^cite_note-Montinaro2014-275] Zakharia et al. (2009) found a similar proportion of Yoruba-like ancestry in their African American samples, with a minority also drawn from [Mandenka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandinka_people) and [Bantu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bantu_peoples) populations. Additionally, the researchers observed an average European ancestry of 21.9%, again with significant variation between individuals.[^cite_note-Zakharia2009-267] Bryc et al. (2009) note that populations from other parts of the continent may also constitute adequate proxies for the ancestors of some African American individuals; namely, ancestral populations from [Guinea Bissau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guinea_Bissau), [Senegal](27278.md) and [Sierra Leone](27308.md) in West Africa and [Angola](701.md) in Southern Africa.[^cite_note-Bryc2009-268] An individual African American person can have over fifteen African ethnic groups in their genetic makeup alone due to the slave trade covering such vast areas.[^cite_note-276]&#x20;

Altogether, genetic studies suggest that African Americans are a genetically diverse people. According to DNA analysis led in 2006 by [Penn State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_State_University) geneticist [Mark D. Shriver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_D._Shriver), around 58 percent of African Americans have at least 12.5% European ancestry (equivalent to one European great-grandparent and their forebears), 19.6 percent of African Americans have at least 25% European ancestry (equivalent to one European grandparent and their forebears), and one percent of African Americans have at least 50% European ancestry (equivalent to one European parent and their forebears).[^cite_note-auto-277][^cite_note-278] According to Shriver, around five percent of African Americans also have at least 12.5% Native American ancestry (equivalent to one Native American great-grandparent and their forebears).[^cite_note-279][^cite_note-280] Research suggests that Native American ancestry among people who identify as African American is a result of relationships that occurred soon after slave ships arrived in the American colonies, and European ancestry is of more recent origin, often from the decades before the Civil War.[^cite_note-dnana1-281]&#x20;

### Y-DNA

Africans bearing the [E-V38](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_E-V38) (E1b1a) likely traversed across the [Sahara](325363.md), from [east](260987.md) to [west](67393.md), approximately 19,000 years ago.[^cite_note-Shrine-282] [E-M2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_E-M2) (E1b1a1) likely originated in West Africa or Central Africa.[^cite_note-Trombetta-283] According to a [Y-DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y_chromosome) study by Sims et al. (2007), the majority (≈60%) of African Americans belong to various subclades of the [E-M2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_E-M2) (E1b1a1, formerly E3a) paternal haplogroup. This is the most common genetic paternal lineage found today among West/Central African males and is also a signature of the historical [Bantu migrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bantu_migration). The next most frequent Y-DNA haplogroup observed among African Americans is the [R1b](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_R1b_\(Y-DNA\)) clade, which around 15% of African Americans carry. This lineage is most common today among Northwestern European males. The remaining African Americans mainly belong to the paternal [haplogroup I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_I-M170) (≈7%), which is also frequent in Northwestern Europe.[^cite_note-Sims2007-284]&#x20;

### mtDNA

According to an [mtDNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitochondrial_DNA) study by Salas et al. (2005), the maternal lineages of African Americans are most similar to haplogroups that are today especially common in West Africa (>55%), followed closely by West-Central Africa and Southwestern Africa (<41%). The characteristic West African haplogroups [L1b](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_L1_\(mtDNA\)), [L2b,c,d](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_L2_\(mtDNA\)), and [L3b,d](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_L3_\(mtDNA\)) and West-Central African haplogroups [L1c](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_L1_\(mtDNA\)) and [L3e](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_L3_\(mtDNA\)) in particular occur at high frequencies among African Americans. As with the paternal DNA of African Americans, contributions from other parts of the continent to their maternal gene pool are insignificant.[^cite_note-Salas2005-285]&#x20;

## Racism and social status

See also: [Income inequality in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_inequality_in_the_United_States)

Formal political, economic and social discrimination against minorities has been present throughout American history. Leland T. Saito, Associate Professor of Sociology and American Studies & Ethnicity at the [University of Southern California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Southern_California), writes, "Political rights have been circumscribed by race, class and gender since the founding of the United States, when the right to vote was restricted to White men of property. Throughout the history of the United States, race has been used by Whites for legitimizing and creating difference and social, economic and political exclusion."[^cite_note-Sato-66]&#x20;

Those who economically gained the most from slavery were the [planter class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planter_class), owners of large-scale plantations where large numbers of enslaved Africans were held captive and forced to produce crops to create wealth for a White elite.[^cite_note-plantation-286] Having a prominent role in politics with [eight presidents owning slaves while in office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_presidents_of_the_United_States_who_owned_slaves), upon the end of the Civil War the planter class kept control of their land and remained politically influential, with the [London School of Economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_School_of_Economics) stating, "this persistence in "de facto power" in turn allowed them to block economic reforms, disenfranchise Black voters, and restrict the mobility of workers."[^cite_note-287]&#x20;

Although they have gained a greater degree of social equality since the civil rights movement, African Americans have remained stagnant economically, which has hindered their ability to break into the middle class and beyond. As of 2020, the [racial wealth gap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_inequality_in_the_United_States) between Whites and Blacks remains as large as it was in 1968, with the typical net worth of a White household equivalent to that of 11.5 Black households.[^cite_note-288] Despite this, African Americans have increased employment rates and gained representation in the highest levels of American government in the post–civil rights era.[^cite_note-Brookings-289] However, widespread [racism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racism_against_African_Americans) remains an issue that continues to undermine the development of social status.[^cite_note-Brookings-289][^cite_note-290]&#x20;

Economically, of all the racially Black ethnic groups on the globe, African Americans are the wealthiest and most successful, with one in every fifty African American families being millionaires.[^cite_note-291] This equates in 2023 to approximately 1.79 million African American millionaires in the United States,[^cite_note-292][^cite_note-293] which is more than the number of millionaires in any racially Black country, and many other countries, around the world.&#x20;

### Policing and criminal justice

See also: [Race and crime in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_and_crime_in_the_United_States) and [Racial profiling in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_profiling_in_the_United_States)

In the US, which has the largest per-capita prison population in the world, African Americans are overrepresented as the second-largest population of prison inmates (38%) in 2023, coming second to Whites, who made up 57% of the prison population.[^cite_note-294] According to the [National Registry of Exonerations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Registry_of_Exonerations), Blacks are roughly 7.5 times more likely to be wrongfully convicted of murder in the US than Whites.[^cite_note-295] In 2012, the New York City Police Department detained people more than 500,000 times under the city's [stop-and-frisk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stop-and-frisk) law. Of the total detained, 55% were African-Americans, while Black people made up 20% of the city's population.[^cite_note-Stanford-296]&#x20;



[Al Sharpton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Sharpton) led the [Commitment March: Get Your Knee Off Our Necks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commitment_March:_Get_Your_Knee_Off_Our_Necks) protest on August 28, 2020.

African American males are more likely to be [killed by police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_use_of_deadly_force_in_the_United_States) when compared to other races.[^cite_note-297] This is one of the factors that led to the creation of the [Black Lives Matter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Lives_Matter) movement in 2013.[^cite_note-298] A historical issue in the US where women have weaponized their White privilege in the country by reporting on Black people, often instigating racial violence,[^cite_note-299][^cite_note-300] difficult White women—who have been given a [different name over the centuries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karen_\(slang\)#Origin) by African Americans—calling the police on Black people became widely publicized in 2020.[^cite_note-301][^cite_note-302] According to *The Guardian*, "The specter of [Karen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karen_\(slang\)) persisted as Black Lives Matter protests and civil unrest spread around the country following [Floyd's murder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murder_of_George_Floyd) and reckonings with racism began to roil institutions, toppling careers as well as statues".[^cite_note-303]&#x20;

In the aftermath of the peak Black Lives Matter protests and widespread police reform efforts in the early 2020s, crime rates surged across the nation, especially in predominantly Black neighborhoods. Many cities experienced near-record or record levels of violence and other criminal activity. As a result, numerous municipalities scaled back police reform initiatives and increased funding for law enforcement.[^cite_note-304][^cite_note-305][^cite_note-306]&#x20;

### Social issues

After over 50 years, marriage rates for all Americans began to decline while divorce rates and out-of-wedlock births have climbed.[^cite_note-media.hoover.org-307] These changes have been greatest among African Americans. After more than 70 years of racial parity Black marriage rates began to fall behind Whites.[^cite_note-media.hoover.org-307] [Single-parent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single_parents_in_the_United_States) households have become most common, and according to US census figures released in January 2010, only 38% of Black children lived with both their parents.[^cite_note-308] In 2021, statistics show that over 80% marriages in the African American ethnic group marry within their ethnic group.[^cite_note-309]&#x20;

Black children are overrepresented in the [U.S. foster care system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foster_care_in_the_United_States). In 2017, Black American children made up 14% of the children population but 22% of those in the foster care system.[^cite_note-310] Black American children are more than twice as likely to be placed in foster care than White American children.[^cite_note-311]&#x20;



Although the [ban on interracial marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-miscegenation_laws) ended in California in 1948, entertainer [Sammy Davis Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sammy_Davis_Jr.) faced a backlash for his involvement with a White woman in 1957.&#x20;

The first ever [anti-miscegenation law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-miscegenation_law) was passed by the [Maryland General Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maryland_General_Assembly) in 1691, criminalizing [interracial marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interracial_marriage).[^cite_note-Anti-miscegenation-312] In a speech in [Charleston, Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charleston,_Illinois) in 1858, [Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln) stated, "I am not, nor ever have been in favor of making voters or jurors of negroes, nor of qualifying them to hold office, nor to intermarry with white people".[^cite_note-313] By the late 1800s, 38 US states had anti-miscegenation statutes.[^cite_note-Anti-miscegenation-312] By 1924, the ban on interracial marriage was still in force in 29 states.[^cite_note-Anti-miscegenation-312] While interracial marriage had been legal in California since 1948, in 1957 actor [Sammy Davis Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sammy_Davis_Jr.) faced a backlash for his involvement with White actress [Kim Novak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Novak).[^cite_note-Smithsonian-314] [Harry Cohn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Cohn), the president of Columbia Pictures, with whom Novak was under contract, gave in to his concerns that a racist backlash against the relationship could hurt the studio.[^cite_note-Smithsonian-314] Davis briefly married Black dancer Loray White in 1958 to protect himself from mob violence.[^cite_note-Smithsonian-314] Inebriated at the wedding ceremony, Davis despairingly said to his best friend, Arthur Silber Jr., "Why won't they let me live my life?" The couple never lived together, and commenced divorce proceedings in September 1958.[^cite_note-Smithsonian-314] In 1958, officers in [Virginia](32432.md) entered the home of [Mildred and Richard Loving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loving_v._Virginia#Plaintiffs) and dragged them out of bed for living together as an interracial couple, on the basis that "any white person intermarry with a colored person"—or vice versa—each party "shall be guilty of a felony" and face prison terms of five years.[^cite_note-Anti-miscegenation-312] In 1967 the law was ruled unconstitutional (via the [14th Amendment](31666.md) adopted in 1868) by the US Supreme Court in *[Loving v. Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loving_v._Virginia)*.[^cite_note-Anti-miscegenation-312]&#x20;

In 2008, Democrats overwhelmingly voted 70% against [California Proposition 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_Proposition_8), African Americans voted 58% in favor of it while 42% voted against Proposition 8.[^cite_note-315] On May 9, 2012, Barack Obama, the first Black president, became the first US president to support same-sex marriage. Since Obama's endorsement there has been a rapid growth in support for same-sex marriage among African Americans. As of 2012, 59% of African Americans support same-sex marriage, which is higher than support among the national average (53%) and White Americans (50%).[^cite_note-316]&#x20;

Polls in [North Carolina](21650.md),[^cite_note-317] [Pennsylvania](23332.md),[^cite_note-318] [Missouri](19571.md),[^cite_note-319] [Maryland](18858.md),[^cite_note-320] [Ohio](22199.md),[^cite_note-321] Florida,[^cite_note-322] and [Nevada](21216.md)[^cite_note-323] have also shown an increase in support for same sex marriage among African Americans. On November 6, 2012, [Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maryland_Question_6), [Maine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maine_Question_1,_2012), and [Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Referendum_74) all voted for approve of same-sex marriage, along with Minnesota rejecting a [constitutional amendment banning same-sex marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Amendment_1). Exit polls in Maryland show about 50% of African Americans voted for same-sex marriage, showing a vast evolution among African Americans on the issue and was crucial in helping pass same-sex marriage in Maryland.[^cite_note-324]&#x20;

Black Americans hold far more conservative opinions on abortion, [extramarital sex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affair), and raising children out of wedlock than Democrats as a whole.[^cite_note-social-325] On financial issues, however, African Americans are in line with Democrats, generally supporting a more [progressive tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_tax) structure to provide more government spending on social services.[^cite_note-326]&#x20;

### Political legacy



Dr. [Martin Luther King Jr.](20076.md) remains the most prominent political leader in the American civil rights movement and perhaps the most influential African American political figure in general.

[African Americans have fought in every war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_African_Americans) in the [history of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_the_United_States).[^cite_note-327]&#x20;

The gains made by African Americans in the [civil rights movement](49001.md) and in the [Black Power movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Power_movement) not only obtained certain rights for African Americans but changed American society in far-reaching and fundamentally important ways. Prior to the 1950s, Black Americans in the South were subject to de jure discrimination, or [Jim Crow laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Crow_laws). They were often the victims of extreme cruelty and violence, sometimes resulting in deaths: by the post World War II era, African Americans became increasingly discontented with their long-standing inequality. In the words of [Martin Luther King Jr.](20076.md), African Americans and their supporters challenged the nation to "rise up and live out the true meaning of its creed that all men are created equal ..."[^cite_note-328]&#x20;

The civil rights movement marked an enormous change in American social, political, economic and civic life. It brought with it [boycotts](18591996.md), [sit-ins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sit-in), [nonviolent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonviolent) demonstrations and marches, court battles, bombings and other violence; prompted worldwide media coverage and intense public debate; forged enduring civic, economic and religious alliances; and disrupted and realigned the nation's two major political parties.&#x20;

Over time, it has changed in fundamental ways the manner in which Blacks and Whites interact with and relate to one another. The movement resulted in the removal of codified, *de jure* racial segregation and discrimination from American life and law, and heavily influenced other groups and movements in struggles for civil rights and social equality within American society, including the [Free Speech Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Speech_Movement), the [disabled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disability), the [women's movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feminist_movement), and [migrant workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Migrant_workers). It also inspired the [Native American rights movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_rights_movement), and in King's 1964 book *[Why We Can't Wait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Why_We_Can%27t_Wait)* he wrote the US "was born in genocide when it embraced the doctrine that the original American, the Indian, was an inferior race."[^cite_note-329][^cite_note-330]&#x20;

## Media and coverage

See also: [Representation of African Americans in media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Representation_of_African_Americans_in_media) and [African-American newspapers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_newspapers)



BET founder [Robert L. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_L._Johnson) with former US President [George W. Bush](3414021.md)

Some activists and academics contend that American news media coverage of African American news, concerns, or dilemmas is inadequate,[^cite_note-331][^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333] or that the news media present distorted images of African Americans.[^cite_note-334]&#x20;

To combat this, [Robert L. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_L._Johnson) founded Black Entertainment Television ([BET](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BET)), a network that targets young African Americans and urban audiences in the United States. Over the years, the network has aired such programming as [rap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip-hop) and [R\&B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contemporary_R%26B) music videos, urban-oriented movies and television series, and some public affairs programs. On Sunday mornings, BET would broadcast Christian programming; the network would also broadcast non-affiliated Christian programs during the early morning hours daily. According to [Viacom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viacom_\(2005%E2%80%932019\)), BET is now a global network that reaches households in the United States, Caribbean, Canada, and the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-335] The network has gone on to spawn several spin-off channels, including [BET Her](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BET_Her) (originally launched as *BET on Jazz*).[^cite_note-336]&#x20;

Another network targeting African Americans is [TV One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TV_One_\(Radio_One\)). TV One is owned by [Urban One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_One), founded and controlled by [Catherine Hughes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathy_Hughes). Urban One is one of the nation's largest radio broadcasting companies and the largest African American-owned radio broadcasting company in the United States.[^cite_note-337]&#x20;

In June 2009, [NBC News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBC_News) launched a new website named [TheGrio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TheGrio).[^cite_note-338] It is the first African American video [news site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/News_site) that focuses on underrepresented stories in existing national news.[^cite_note-339]&#x20;

### Black-owned and oriented media outlets

- [The Africa Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Africa_Channel) – Dedicated to programming about African culture.

- [aspireTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aspire_\(TV_network\)) – a digital cable and satellite channel owned by businessman and former basketball player [Magic Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magic_Johnson).

- ATTV – an independent public affairs and educational channel.

- [BET Media Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BET_Media_Group) – The most prominent multimedia outlet targeting Afro-Americans.&#x20;

  - [BET](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BET)

  - [BET Her](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BET_Her)

  - [VH1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VH1) – Originally a [MTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MTV) spin-off focused on light genres of music, the network's programming became slanted towards African American culture during the 2010s.

    [^cite_note-340]

- [Bounce TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bounce_TV) – a digital multicast network owned by the [E. W. Scripps Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E._W._Scripps_Company).

- [Fox Soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_Soul) – a digital television and streaming network primarily airing original talk shows and syndicated programming

- [Oprah Winfrey Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oprah_Winfrey_Network) – a cable and satellite network founded by [Oprah Winfrey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oprah_Winfrey) and jointly owned by [Warner Bros. Discovery](67824355.md) and [Harpo Studios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harpo_Studios). While not exclusively targeting African Americans, much of its original programming is geared towards a similar demographic.

- [Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolt_\(TV_network\)) – a music channel and media company founded by [Sean "Puff Daddy" Combs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sean_Combs).

- [Soul of the South Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul_of_the_South_Network) – a regional broadcast network.

- [TheGrio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TheGrio) – a digital multicast network focused on news and opinion-based programming.

- [TV One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TV_One_\(U.S._TV_network\)) – a general entertainment network targeting adults.&#x20;

  - [Cleo TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TV_One_\(American_TV_channel\)#Cleo_TV) – a sister network targeting [millennial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millennial) and [Generation X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generation_X) women

- [We TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/We_TV) – Owned by [AMC Networks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AMC_Networks), became slanted towards Black women during the 2010s

## Culture

Further information: [African-American culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_culture)

See also: [African-American art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_art)



A traditional [soul food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul_food) dinner consisting of [fried chicken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fried_chicken) with [macaroni and cheese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macaroni_and_cheese), [collard greens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collard_greens), breaded [fried okra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fried_okra), and [cornbread](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornbread)

From their earliest presence in North America, African Americans have significantly contributed literature, art, agricultural skills, cuisine, clothing styles, music, language, and social and technological innovation to American culture. The cultivation and use of many agricultural products in the United States, such as [yams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweet_potato), peanuts, rice, [okra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Okra), [sorghum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sorghum), [grits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grits), [watermelon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watermelon), [indigo dyes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigo_dye), and cotton, can be traced to West African and African American influences. Notable examples include [George Washington Carver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington_Carver), who created nearly 500 products from peanuts, sweet potatoes, and pecans.[^cite_note-341] [Soul food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul_food) is a variety of cuisine popular among African Americans. It is closely related to the [cuisine of the Southern United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuisine_of_the_Southern_United_States). The descriptive terminology may have originated in the mid-1960s, when *[soul](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/soul)* was a common definer used to describe African American culture (for example, [soul music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul_music)). African Americans were the first peoples in the United States to make fried chicken, along with [Scottish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_people) immigrants to the South. Although the Scottish had been frying chicken before they emigrated, they lacked the spices and flavor that African Americans had used when preparing the meal. The Scottish American settlers therefore adopted the African American method of seasoning chicken.[^cite_note-342] However, fried chicken was generally a rare meal in the African American community and was usually reserved for special events or celebrations.[^cite_note-343]&#x20;

### Language

Main article: [African-American English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_English)

See also: [Black American Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_American_Sign_Language), [Gullah language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gullah_language), [Afro-Seminole Creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Seminole_Creole), and [Louisiana Creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Creole)

[African-American English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_English) is a [variety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variety_\(linguistics\)) ([dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dialect), [ethnolect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnolect), and [sociolect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociolect)) of [American English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_English), commonly spoken by urban [working-class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Working_class) and largely [bi-dialectal](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/bidialectal) [middle-class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_class) African Americans.[^cite_note-344] It shares parts of its [grammar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammar) and [phonology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phonology) with the [Southern American English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_American_English) dialect. African American English differs from Standard American English (SAE) in certain pronunciation characteristics, tense usage, and grammatical structures, which were derived from West African languages (particularly those belonging to the [Niger–Congo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niger%E2%80%93Congo_languages) family).[^cite_note-Aave-345]&#x20;

Virtually all habitual speakers of African American English can understand and communicate in Standard American English. As with all linguistic forms, AAVE's usage is influenced by various factors, including geographical, educational and socioeconomic background, as well as formality of setting.[^cite_note-Aave-345] Additionally, there are many literary uses of this variety of English, particularly in [African American literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_literature).[^cite_note-346]&#x20;

Other languages are spoken by specific sub-communities. The [Gullah language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gullah_language) is an English-based [creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creole_language) language spoken mostly in the coastal regions of [South Carolina](27956.md) and [Georgia](48830.md) by the [Gullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gullah);[^cite_note-cross-347] an off-shoot of this is [Afro-Seminole Creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Seminole_Creole) spoken by [Black Seminoles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Seminoles) mostly now in [Mexico](3966054.md) and [Brackettville, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brackettville,_Texas).[^cite_note-348] [Louisiana Creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Creole) is a [French-based creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French-based_creole_languages) and spoken mostly in [Louisiana](18130.md).[^cite_note-349]&#x20;

#### Traditional names

Main article: [African-American names](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_names)

[African-American names](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_names) are part of the cultural traditions of African Americans, most of these cultural names having no connection to Africa but strictly an African American cultural practice that developed in the United States during enslavement.[^cite_note-Logan-350] This new evidence became apparent by census records which show African Americans and White Americans, though they spoke the same language, chose to use different names even during times of enslavement, which is where and when the development of African American cultural names began.[^cite_note-Logan-350]&#x20;

Prior to this newer information, it was only thought that before the 1950s, and 1960s, most African-American names closely resembled those used within European-American culture.[^cite_note-Norman-351] Babies of that era were generally given a few common names, with children using nicknames to distinguish the various people with the same name. With the rise of 1960s civil rights movement, there was a dramatic increase in names of various origins.[^cite_note-Moskowitz-352]&#x20;

By the 1970s, and 1980s, it had become common among African Americans to invent new names for themselves, although many of these invented names took elements from popular existing names. Prefixes such as La/Le, Da/De, Ra/Re and Ja/Je, and suffixes like -ique/iqua, -isha and -aun/-awn are common, as are inventive spellings for common names. The book *Baby Names Now: From Classic to Cool—The Very Last Word on First Names* places the origins of "La" names in African-American culture in [New Orleans](53842.md).[^cite_note-Rosenkrantz-353]&#x20;

Even with the rise of inventive names, it is still common for African Americans to use biblical, historical, or traditional European names. Daniel, Christopher, Michael, David, James, Joseph, and Matthew were thus among the most frequent names for African-American boys in 2013.[^cite_note-Norman-351][^cite_note-354][^cite_note-Conley-355]&#x20;

The name LaKeisha is typically considered American in origin but has elements that were drawn from both French and West/Central African roots. Names such as LaTanisha, JaMarcus, DeAndre, and Shaniqua were created in the same way. Punctuation marks are seen more often within African American names than other American names, such as the names Mo'nique and D'Andre.[^cite_note-Norman-351]&#x20;

### Music



The King & Carter [Jazzing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz) Orchestra photographed in Houston, Texas, January 1921



[Chuck Berry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chuck_Berry) is considered a pioneer of [rock and roll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_and_roll).

[African American music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_music) is one of the most pervasive African American cultural influences in the United States today and is among the most dominant in mainstream popular music. [Hip-hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip-hop), [R\&B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhythm_and_blues), [funk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funk), [rock and roll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_and_roll), [soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul_music), [blues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blues), and other contemporary American musical forms originated in Black communities and evolved from other Black forms of music, including blues, [doo-wop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doo-wop), [barbershop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbershop_music), [ragtime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ragtime), [bluegrass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bluegrass_music), [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz), and [gospel music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_music).&#x20;

African American-derived musical forms have also influenced and been incorporated into virtually every other [popular music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_music) genre in the world, including [country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Country_music) and [techno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Techno). African American genres are the most important ethnic vernacular tradition in America, as they have developed independent of African traditions from which they arise more so than any other immigrant groups, including Europeans; make up the broadest and longest lasting range of styles in America; and have, historically, been more influential, interculturally, geographically, and economically, than other American vernacular traditions.[^cite_note-stewart-356]&#x20;

### Dance

African Americans have also had an important role in American dance. [Bill T. Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_T._Jones), a prominent modern choreographer and dancer, has included historical African American themes in his work, particularly in the piece "Last Supper at Uncle Tom's Cabin/The Promised Land". Likewise, [Alvin Ailey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alvin_Ailey)'s artistic work, including his "Revelations" based on his experience growing up as an African American in the South during the 1930s, has had a significant influence on modern dance. Another form of dance, [stepping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stepping_\(African-American\)), is an African American tradition whose performance and competition has been formalized through the traditionally Black fraternities and sororities at universities.[^cite_note-Anderson-357]&#x20;

### Sports

This section is an excerpt from [African Americans in sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Americans_in_sports).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=African_Americans_in_sports\&action=edit)]

Discussions of [race and sports in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_and_sports_in_the_United_States), where the two subjects have always been intertwined in [American history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_history), have focused to a great extent on African Americans. Depending on the type of sport and performance level, African Americans are reported to be over- or under-represented. African Americans compose the highest percentage of the minority groups active at the professional level, but are among those who show the lowest participation overall. And though the list of African Americans in professional sports remains high, it only represents a small fraction of aspiring black athletes.&#x20;

### Literature and academics



[Toni Morrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toni_Morrison), recipient of the Nobel Prize for literature

Many African American authors have written stories, poems, and essays influenced by their experiences as African Americans. [African American literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_literature) is a major genre in American literature. Famous examples include [Langston Hughes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Langston_Hughes), [James Baldwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Baldwin_\(writer\)), [Richard Wright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Wright_\(author\)), [Zora Neale Hurston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zora_Neale_Hurston), [Ralph Ellison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ralph_Ellison), Nobel Prize winner [Toni Morrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toni_Morrison), and [Maya Angelou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maya_Angelou).&#x20;

[African American inventors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_inventor) have created many widely used devices in the world and have contributed to international [innovation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Innovation). [Norbert Rillieux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norbert_Rillieux) created the technique for converting sugar cane juice into white sugar crystals. Moreover, Rillieux left [Louisiana](18130.md) in 1854 and went to France, where he spent ten years working with the Champollions deciphering [Egyptian hieroglyphics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_hieroglyphs) from the [Rosetta Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosetta_Stone).[^cite_note-358] Most slave inventors were nameless, such as the slave owned by the [Confederate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_States_of_America) President [Jefferson Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jefferson_Davis) who designed the ship propeller used by the Confederate navy.[^cite_note-359]&#x20;

By 1913, over 1,000 inventions were patented by Black Americans. Among the most notable inventors were [Jan Matzeliger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Matzeliger), who developed the first machine to mass-produce shoes,[^cite_note-360] and [Elijah McCoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elijah_McCoy), who invented automatic lubrication devices for steam engines.[^cite_note-361] [Granville Woods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Granville_Woods) had 35 patents to improve electric railway systems, including the first system to allow moving trains to communicate.[^cite_note-362] [Garrett A. Morgan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garrett_A._Morgan) developed the first automatic traffic signal and gas mask.[^cite_note-363]&#x20;

[Lewis Howard Latimer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewis_Howard_Latimer) invented an improvement for the incandescent light bulb.[^cite_note-364] More recent inventors include [Frederick McKinley Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_McKinley_Jones), who invented the movable refrigeration unit for food transport in trucks and trains.[^cite_note-FMJones-365] [Lloyd Quarterman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lloyd_Quarterman) worked with six other Black scientists on the creation of the atomic bomb (code named the [Manhattan Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manhattan_Project))[^cite_note-366] and helped develop the first nuclear reactor.[^cite_note-367]&#x20;

As part of the preservation of their culture, African Americans have continuously launched their own publications and publishing houses, such as [Robert Sengstacke Abbott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Sengstacke_Abbott), founder of the Chicago Defender newspaper, and [Carter G. Woodson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carter_G._Woodson), the founder of [Black History Month](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_History_Month) who spent over thirty years documenting and publishing African American history in journals and books.[^cite_note-368] The [Johnson Publishing Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnson_Publishing_Company), founded by [John H. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_H._Johnson) in 1942, is a National Historic Landmark.[^cite_note-369]&#x20;

## Terminology

### General



This parade float displayed the word "Afro-Americans" in 1911.

The term *African American* was popularized by [Jesse Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesse_Jackson) in the 1980s,[^cite_note-wilkersonnyt-7] although there are recorded uses from the 18th and 19th centuries,[^cite_note-370] for example, in post-emancipation holidays and conferences.[^cite_note-371][^cite_note-372] Earlier terms also used to describe Americans of African ancestry referred more to skin color than to ancestry. Other terms (such as *[colored](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colored)*, *[person of color](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Person_of_color)*, or *[negro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negro)*) were included in the wording of various laws and legal decisions which some thought were being used as tools of [White supremacy](33178.md) and [oppression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oppression).[^cite_note-books.google.com-373]&#x20;



[Michelle Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelle_Obama) was the [First Lady of the United States](10847.md); she and her husband, President Barack Obama, are the first African Americans to hold these positions.

A 16-page pamphlet entitled "A Sermon on the Capture of Lord Cornwallis" is notable for the attribution of its authorship to "An *African American*". Published in 1782, the book's use of this phrase predates any other yet identified by more than 50 years.[^cite_note-374]&#x20;

In the 1980s, the term *African American* was advanced to give descendants of [American slaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States), and other American Blacks who lived through the slavery era, a [heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_heritage) and a cultural base.[^cite_note-books.google.com-373] The term was popularized in Black communities around the country via [word of mouth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Word_of_mouth) and ultimately received mainstream use after [Jesse Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesse_Jackson)'s use in 1988. Subsequently, major media outlets adopted it.[^cite_note-books.google.com-373]&#x20;

Surveys in the 1990s and the first decade of the 21st century showed that the majority of Black Americans had no preference for *African American* versus *Black American*,[^cite_note-375] although they had a slight preference for the latter in personal settings and the former in more formal settings.[^cite_note-376] By 2021, according to polling from [Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup,_Inc.), 58% of Black Americans expressed no preference for what their group should be called, with 17% each preferring *Black* and *African-American*. Among those with no preference, Gallup found a slight majority favored *Black* "if \[they] had to choose."[^cite_note-377]&#x20;

In 2020, the [Associated Press](18935732.md) updated its [AP Stylebook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AP_Stylebook) to direct its writers to capitalize the first letter of *Black* when it is used "in a racial, ethnic or cultural sense, conveying an essential and shared sense of history, identity and community among people who identify as Black, including those in the African diaspora and within Africa."[^cite_note-378] *[The New York Times](30680.md)* and other outlets made similar changes at the same time.[^cite_note-379]&#x20;

In 2023, the government released a new more detailed breakdown due to the rise in racially Black immigration into the US, listing African American as a compound termed ethnicity, distinguished from other racially Black ethnicities such as Nigerian, Jamaican etc.[^cite_note-380]&#x20;

The term *African American* embraces [pan-Africanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Africanism) as earlier enunciated by prominent African thinkers such as [Marcus Garvey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcus_Garvey), [W. E. B. Du Bois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W._E._B._Du_Bois), and [George Padmore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Padmore). The term *Afro-[Usonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Usonia)*, and variations of such, are more rarely used.[^cite_note-381][^cite_note-382]&#x20;

### Official identity



[Racially segregated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racially_segregated) Negro section of keypunch operators at the [US Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Census_Bureau)

Since 1977, in an attempt to keep up with changing social opinion, the [United States government](195149.md) has officially classified Black people (revised to *Black* or *African American* in 1997) as "having origins in any of the Black racial groups of Africa."[^cite_note-censusblack-383] Other federal offices, such as the US Census Bureau, adhere to the [Office of Management and Budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_Management_and_Budget) standards on race in their data collection and tabulation efforts.[^cite_note-OMB-384] In preparation for the [2010 US census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_United_States_census), a marketing and outreach plan called *2010 Census Integrated Communications Campaign Plan* (ICC) recognized and defined African Americans as Black people born in the United States. From the ICC perspective, African Americans are one of three groups of Black people in the United States: African Americans, Black Africans, and Afro-Caribbeans.[^cite_note-US2010ICCBlkAud-385]&#x20;

The ICC plan was to reach the three groups by acknowledging that each group has its own sense of community that is based on geography and ethnicity.[^cite_note-US2010ICCBstRch-386] The best way to market the census process toward any of the three groups is to reach them through their own unique communication channels and not treat the entire Black population of the US as though they are all African Americans with a single ethnic and geographical background. The [Federal Bureau of Investigation](11127.md) of the [US Department of Justice](52563.md) categorizes Black or African American people as "\[a] person having origins in any of the Black racial groups of Africa" through racial categories used in the UCR Program adopted from the Statistical Policy Handbook (1978) and published by the Office of Federal Statistical Policy and Standards, [US Department of Commerce](70243.md), derived from the 1977 [Office of Management and Budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_Management_and_Budget) classification.[^cite_note-FBIpop-387]&#x20;

### Admixture

See also: [Interracial marriage in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interracial_marriage_in_the_United_States), [Miscegenation § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miscegenation#United_States), [Multiracial American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiracial_American), [One-drop rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One-drop_rule), and [hypodescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypodescent)

Historically, "[race mixing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miscegenation)" between Black and White people was [taboo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taboo) in the United States. So-called [anti-miscegenation laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-miscegenation_laws), barring Blacks and Whites from [marrying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interracial_marriage_in_the_United_States) or having sex, were established in [colonial America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_America) as early as 1691,[^cite_note-388] and endured in many [Southern states](179553.md) until the [Supreme Court](31737.md) ruled them unconstitutional in *[Loving v. Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loving_v._Virginia)* (1967). The taboo among American Whites surrounding White-Black relations is a historical consequence of the oppression and [racial segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation) of African Americans.[^cite_note-389] Historian [David Brion Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Brion_Davis) notes the racial mixing that occurred during slavery was frequently attributed by the [planter class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plantations_in_the_American_South) to the "lower-class white males" but Davis concludes that "there is abundant evidence that many slaveowners, sons of slaveowners, and overseers took Black mistresses or in effect raped the wives and daughters of slave families."[^cite_note-390] A famous example was [Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson)'s mistress, [Sally Hemings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sally_Hemings).[^cite_note-391] Although publicly opposed to race mixing, Jefferson, in his *[Notes on the State of Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notes_on_the_State_of_Virginia)* published in 1785, wrote: "The improvement of the Blacks in body and mind, in the first instance of their mixture with the whites, has been observed by every one, and proves that their inferiority is not the effect merely of their condition of life".[^cite_note-392]&#x20;

[Harvard University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard_University) historian [Henry Louis Gates Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Louis_Gates_Jr.) wrote in 2009 that "African Americans...are a racially mixed or [mulatto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mulatto) people—deeply and overwhelmingly so". After the [Emancipation Proclamation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emancipation_Proclamation), [Chinese American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Americans) men married African American women in high proportions to their total marriage numbers due to few Chinese American women being in the United States.[^cite_note-The_United_States-393] African slaves and their descendants have also had a history of cultural exchange and [intermarriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miscegenation) with Native Americans,[^cite_note-gen2-394] although they did not necessarily retain social, cultural or linguistic ties to Native peoples.[^cite_note-sad-395] There are also increasing intermarriages and offspring between non-Hispanic Blacks and Hispanics of any race, especially between [Puerto Ricans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Ricans) and African Americans.[^cite_note-396]&#x20;

Racially mixed marriages have become increasingly accepted in the United States since the civil rights movement.[^cite_note-Swanbrow-397] Approval in national opinion polls has risen from 36% in 1978, to 48% in 1991, 65% in 2002, 77% in 2007.[^cite_note-398] A Gallup poll conducted in 2013 found that 84% of Whites and 96% of Blacks approved of interracial marriage, and 87% overall.[^cite_note-399] Black men are more than twice as likely to [date and marry interracially than Black women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interracial_marriage_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-400]&#x20;

At the end of World War II, some African American military men stationed in [Asia](689.md) and [Europe](9239.md) impregnated local non-Black women, resulting in the birth of thousands of mixed-race children called "[Brown Babies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_Babies)". Some of these families later immigrated to the United States. However, some mixed-race children, especially in [Japan](15573.md), were placed in orphanages due to social stigmas associated with half-Black fatherless children in the 1950s.[^cite_note-401][^cite_note-402][^cite_note-403]&#x20;

### Terminology dispute
