Author [Debra Dickerson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debra_Dickerson) has argued that the term *[Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_people)* should refer strictly to the descendants of Africans who were brought to America as slaves, and not to the sons and daughters of Black immigrants who lack that ancestry. Thus, under her definition, President Barack Obama, who is the son of a Kenyan, is not Black.[^cite_note-colorblind-salon-404][^cite_note-colbertnation.com-405] She makes the argument that grouping all people of African descent together regardless of their unique ancestral circumstances would inevitably deny the lingering effects of slavery within the American community of slave descendants, in addition to denying Black immigrants recognition of their own unique ancestral backgrounds. "Lumping us all together", Dickerson wrote, "erases the significance of slavery and continuing racism while giving the appearance of progress."[^cite_note-colorblind-salon-404] Similar comments have been made concerning Kamala Harris, the daughter of a Jamaican immigrant, who was elected vice president in 2020.[^cite_note-Libactivists-406][^cite_note-\:1-407][^cite_note-\:2-408]&#x20;

Similar viewpoints to Dickerson's have been expressed by author [Stanley Crouch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanley_Crouch) in a *[New York Daily News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Daily_News)* piece, [Charles Steele Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Steele_Jr.) of the [Southern Christian Leadership Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Christian_Leadership_Conference)[^cite_note-409] and African American columnist [David Ehrenstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Ehrenstein) of the *[Los Angeles Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Times)*, who accused White liberals of flocking to Blacks who were *[Magic Negros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magic_Negro)*, a term that refers to a Black person with no past who simply appears to assist the mainstream White (as cultural protagonists/drivers) agenda.[^cite_note-Obama_the_'Magic_Negro'-410] Ehrenstein went on to say "He's there to assuage white 'guilt' they feel over the role of slavery and racial segregation in American history."[^cite_note-Obama_the_'Magic_Negro'-410]&#x20;

The [American Descendants of Slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Descendants_of_Slavery) (ADOS) movement coalesces around this view, arguing that Black descendants of American slavery deserve a separate ethnic category that distinguishes them from other Black groups in the United States.[^cite_note-NY_Times_overview-411] Their terminology has gained popularity in some circles, but others have criticized the movement for a perceived bias against (especially poor and Black) immigrants, and for its often inflammatory rhetoric.[^cite_note-Libactivists-406][^cite_note-412][^cite_note-413] Politicians such as Obama and Harris have received especially pointed criticism from the movement, as neither are ADOS and have spoken out at times against policies specific to them.[^cite_note-\:1-407][^cite_note-\:2-408]&#x20;

Many [Pan-African](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Africanism) movements and organizations that are ideologically [Black nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_nationalism), [anti-imperialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-imperialism), [anti-Zionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Zionism), and [Scientific socialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_socialism) like The [All-African People's Revolutionary Party (A-APRP)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-African_People%27s_Revolutionary_Party), have argued that [African](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_diaspora) (relating to the diaspora) or [New Afrikan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_New_Afrika) should be used instead of African American.[^cite_note-414] Most notably, [Malcolm X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malcolm_X) and [Kwame Ture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stokely_Carmichael) expressed similar views that African Americans are Africans who "happen to be in America", and should not claim or identify as being American if they are fighting for Black (New Afrikan) liberation. Historically, this is due to the enslavement of Africans during the [transatlantic slave trade](49005.md), ongoing anti-Black violence, and structural racism in countries like the United States.[^cite_note-415][^cite_note-416]&#x20;

### Terms no longer in common use

Before the independence of the [Thirteen Colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteen_Colonies) until the abolition of slavery in 1865, an African American slave was commonly known as a *[negro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negro)*. *[Free negro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_negro)* was the legal status in the territory of an African American person who was not enslaved.[^cite_note-417] In response to the project of the [American Colonization Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Colonization_Society) to transport free Blacks to the future Liberia, a project most Blacks strongly rejected, the Blacks at the time said they were no more African than White Americans were European, and referred to themselves with what they considered a more acceptable term, "[colored](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colored) Americans". The term was used until the second quarter of the 20th century, when it was considered outmoded – although it was retained in the name of the [National Association for the Advancement of Colored People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAACP) (NAACP) – and generally gave way again to the exclusive use of *negro*. By the 1940s, the term was commonly capitalized (*Negro*); but by the mid-1960s, it was considered disparaging. By the end of the 20th century, *negro* had come to be considered inappropriate and was rarely used and perceived as a [pejorative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pejorative).[^cite_note-418][^cite_note-419] The term is rarely used by younger Black people, but remained in use by many older African Americans who had grown up with the term, particularly in the Southern US.[^cite_note-420] The term remains in use in some contexts, such as the [United Negro College Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Negro_College_Fund), an American philanthropic organization.&#x20;

There are many other deliberately insulting terms, many of which were in common use (e.g., *[nigger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigger)*), but had become unacceptable in normal discourse before the end of the 20th century. One exception is the use, among the Black community, of the slur *nigger* rendered as *[nigga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigga)*, representing the pronunciation of the word in [African American English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_English). This usage has been popularized by American [rap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rap) and [hip-hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip-hop) [music cultures](171080.md) and is used as part of an [in-group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In-group_and_out-group) [lexicon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lexicon) and speech. It is not necessarily [derogatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pejorative) and, when used among Black people, the word is often used to mean "[homie](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/homie)" or "friend".[^cite_note-421] Acceptance of intra-group usage of the word *nigga* is still debated, although it has established a foothold among younger generations. The [NAACP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAACP) denounces the use of both *nigga* and *nigger*.[^cite_note-BaltSun-422]&#x20;

"Black or African Americans" refers to a person having origins in any of the Black racial groups of Africa. The Black racial category includes people who marked the "Black, African Am., or Negro" checkbox. It also includes respondents who reported entries such as African American; Sub-Saharan African entries, such as Kenyan and Nigerian; and Afro-Caribbean entries, such as Haitian and Jamaican."

[^cite_note-5]: &#x20;["Measuring Racial and Ethnic Diversity for the 2020 Census"](https://www.census.gov/newsroom/blogs/random-samplings/2021/08/measuring-racial-ethnic-diversity-2020-census.html). *The United States Census Bureau*. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20230430114126/https://www.census.gov/newsroom/blogs/random-samplings/2021/08/measuring-racial-ethnic-diversity-2020-census.html) from the original on April 30, 2023. Retrieved April 23, 2023.

[^cite_note-Locke-6]: &#x20;Locke, Don C.; Bailey, Deryl F. (2013). [*Increasing Multicultural Understanding*](https://books.google.com/books?id=7nJFBAAAQBAJ\&pg=PA106). Sage Publications. p. 106. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-4833-1421-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-4833-1421-1). [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20180818040343/https://books.google.com/books?id=7nJFBAAAQBAJ\&pg=PA106) from the original on August 18, 2018. Retrieved March 7, 2018. African American refers to descendants of enslaved Black people who are from the United States. The reason we use an entire continent (Africa) instead of a country (e.g., Irish American) is because slave masters purposefully obliterated tribal ancestry, language, and family units in order to destroy the spirit of the people they enslaved, thereby making it impossible for their descendants to trace their history prior to being born into slavery.

[^cite_note-wilkersonnyt-7]: &#x20;Wilkerson, Isabel (January 31, 1989). ["'African-American' Favored By Many of America's Blacks"](https://www.nytimes.com/1989/01/31/us/african-american-favored-by-many-of-america-s-blacks.html). *The New York Times*. [ISSN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISSN_\(identifier\)) [0362-4331](https://search.worldcat.org/issn/0362-4331). Retrieved December 28, 2020. A movement led by the Rev. Jesse Jackson to call blacks African-Americans has met with both rousing approval and deep-seated skepticism in a debate that is coming to symbolize the role and history of blacks in this country. ... The question of a name has caused pain and controversy since the first Africans were captured and shipped to the Americas in the 17th century. The slaves called themselves Africans at first, but slave masters gave them English names and called them Negroes, the Portuguese word for black, historians say. That term was resented by some blacks who said it was degrading when whites mispronounced it, accidentally or intentionally.

Retrieved September 6, 2012. The Black audience includes all individuals of Black African descent. There are three major groups that represent the Black Audience in the United States. These groups are African Americans (Blacks born in the United States), Black Africans (Black Immigrants from Africa) and Afro-Caribbeans, which includes Haitians.

Community, both geographic and ethnic, creates a sense of belonging and pride that is unique to the Black audience (African Americans, Afro-Caribbeans, and Black Africans).
